[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Werner_Heisenberg_-_Canonical_commutation_rule_for_position_and_momentum_variables_of_a_particle_-_Uncertainty_principle%2C_1927.jpg/250px-Werner_Heisenberg_-_Canonical_commutation_rule_for_position_and_momentum_variables_of_a_particle_-_Uncertainty_principle%2C_1927.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Werner_Heisenberg_-_Canonical_commutation_rule_for_position_and_momentum_variables_of_a_particle_-_Uncertainty_principle,_1927.jpg)Canonical commutation rule for position _q_ and momentum _p_ variables of a particle, 1927. _pq_ − _qp_ = _h_ /(2 _πi_). Uncertainty principle of Heisenberg, 1927.
The **uncertainty principle** , also known as **Heisenberg's indeterminacy principle** , is a fundamental concept in [quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_mechanics "Quantum mechanics"). It states that there is a limit to the precision with which certain pairs of physical properties, such as position and [momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Momentum "Momentum"), can be simultaneously known. In other words, the more accurately one property is measured, the less accurately the other property can be known. 
More formally, the uncertainty principle is any of a variety of [mathematical inequalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inequality_\(mathematics\) "Inequality \(mathematics\)") asserting a fundamental limit to the product of the accuracy of certain related pairs of measurements on a quantum system, such as [position](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Position_\(vector\) "Position \(vector\)"), _x_ , and momentum, _p_.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1) Such paired-variables are known as [complementary variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complementarity_\(physics\) "Complementarity \(physics\)") or [canonically conjugate variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_coordinates "Canonical coordinates"). 
First introduced in 1927 by German physicist [Werner Heisenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Werner_Heisenberg "Werner Heisenberg"),[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-:0-2)[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-3)[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-4)[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-5) the formal inequality relating the [standard deviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_deviation "Standard deviation") of position _σ x_ and the standard deviation of momentum _σ p_ was derived by [Earle Hesse Kennard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earle_Hesse_Kennard "Earle Hesse Kennard")[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Kennard-6) later that year and by [Hermann Weyl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermann_Weyl "Hermann Weyl")[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Weyl1928-7) in 1928: 
σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/dfad4605bd5cc074dba10330bd766f60b113fa1b)
where  ℏ = h 2 π {\displaystyle \hbar ={\frac {h}{2\pi }}} ![{\\displaystyle \\hbar ={\\frac {h}{2\\pi }}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/df05180652a87fe1af83af4bba3402117bd18466) is the [reduced Planck constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reduced_Planck_constant "Reduced Planck constant"). 
The quintessentially quantum mechanical uncertainty principle comes in many forms other than position–momentum. The energy–time relationship is widely used to relate quantum state lifetime to measured energy widths but its formal derivation is fraught with confusing issues about the nature of time. The basic principle has been extended in numerous directions; it must be considered in many kinds of fundamental physical measurements. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Position–momentum")]
Main article: [Introduction to quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduction_to_quantum_mechanics "Introduction to quantum mechanics")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/d/db/Sequential_superposition_of_plane_waves.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sequential_superposition_of_plane_waves.gif)The superposition of several plane waves to form a wave packet. This wave packet becomes increasingly localized with the addition of many waves. The Fourier transform is a mathematical operation that separates a wave packet into its individual plane waves. The waves shown here are [real](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_function "Real function") for illustrative purposes only; in quantum mechanics the wave function is generally [complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_function "Complex function").
It is vital to illustrate how the principle applies to relatively intelligible physical situations since it is indiscernible on the macroscopic[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-8) scales that humans experience. Two alternative frameworks for quantum physics offer different explanations for the uncertainty principle. The [wave mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schr%C3%B6dinger_equation "Schrödinger equation") picture of the uncertainty principle is more visually intuitive, but the more abstract [matrix mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrix_mechanics "Matrix mechanics") picture formulates it in a way that generalizes more easily. 
Mathematically, in wave mechanics, the uncertainty relation between position and momentum arises because the expressions of the wavefunction in the two corresponding [orthonormal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthonormal "Orthonormal") [bases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basis_\(linear_algebra\) "Basis \(linear algebra\)") in [Hilbert space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilbert_space "Hilbert space") are [Fourier transforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourier_transforms "Fourier transforms") of one another (i.e., position and momentum are [conjugate variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugate_variables "Conjugate variables")). A nonzero function and its Fourier transform cannot both be sharply localized at the same time.[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-9) A similar tradeoff between the variances of Fourier conjugates arises in all systems underlain by Fourier analysis, for example in sound waves: A pure tone is a [sharp spike](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_delta_function "Dirac delta function") at a single frequency, while its Fourier transform gives the shape of the sound wave in the time domain, which is a completely delocalized sine wave. In quantum mechanics, the two key points are that the position of the particle takes the form of a matter wave, and momentum is its Fourier conjugate, assured by the [de Broglie relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter_wave "Matter wave") _p_ = _ħk_ , where k is the [wavenumber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavenumber "Wavenumber"). 
In [matrix mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrix_mechanics "Matrix mechanics"), the [mathematical formulation of quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_formulation_of_quantum_mechanics#Postulates_of_quantum_mechanics "Mathematical formulation of quantum mechanics"), any pair of non-[commuting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commutator "Commutator") [self-adjoint operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-adjoint_operator "Self-adjoint operator") representing [observables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observable "Observable") are subject to similar uncertainty limits. An eigenstate of an observable represents the state of the wavefunction for a certain measurement value (the eigenvalue). For example, if a measurement of an observable A is performed, then the system is in a particular eigenstate Ψ of that observable. However, the particular eigenstate of the observable A need not be an eigenstate of another observable B: If so, then it does not have a unique associated measurement for it, as the system is not in an eigenstate of that observable.[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-10)
### Visualization
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: Visualization")]
The uncertainty principle can be visualized using the position- and momentum-space wavefunctions for one spinless particle with mass in one dimension. 
The more localized the position-space wavefunction, the more likely the particle is to be found with the position coordinates in that region, and correspondingly the momentum-space wavefunction is less localized so the possible momentum components the particle could have are more widespread. Conversely, the more localized the momentum-space wavefunction, the more likely the particle is to be found with those values of momentum components in that region, and correspondingly the less localized the position-space wavefunction, so the position coordinates the particle could occupy are more widespread. These wavefunctions are [Fourier transforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourier_transform "Fourier transform") of each other: mathematically, the uncertainty principle expresses the relationship between conjugate variables in the transform. 
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Quantum_mechanics_travelling_wavefunctions_wavelength.svg/960px-Quantum_mechanics_travelling_wavefunctions_wavelength.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Quantum_mechanics_travelling_wavefunctions_wavelength.svg)Position _x_ and momentum _p_ wavefunctions corresponding to quantum particles. The colour opacity of the particles corresponds to the [probability density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probability_density "Probability density") of finding the particle with position _x_ or momentum component _p_.  
**Top:** If wavelength _λ_ is unknown, so are momentum _p_ , wave-vector _k_ and energy _E_ (de Broglie relations). As the particle is more localized in position space, Δ _x_ is smaller than for Δ _p x_.  
**Bottom:** If _λ_ is known, so are _p_ , _k_ , and _E_. As the particle is more localized in momentum space, Δ _p_ is smaller than for Δ _x_.
### Wave mechanics interpretation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Wave mechanics interpretation")]
Main articles: [Wave packet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_packet "Wave packet") and [Schrödinger equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schr%C3%B6dinger_equation "Schrödinger equation")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_plane_wave.svg/250px-Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_plane_wave.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_plane_wave.svg)
[Plane wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plane_wave "Plane wave")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_wavepacket.svg/250px-Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_wavepacket.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Propagation_of_a_de_broglie_wavepacket.svg)
[Wave packet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_packet "Wave packet")
Propagation of [de Broglie waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter_wave "Matter wave") in 1d—real part of the [complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_number "Complex number") amplitude is blue, imaginary part is green. The probability (shown as the colour [opacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opacity_\(optics\) "Opacity \(optics\)")) of finding the particle at a given point _x_ is spread out like a waveform, there is no definite position of the particle. As the amplitude increases above zero the [curvature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curvature "Curvature") reverses sign, so the amplitude begins to decrease again, and vice versa—the result is an alternating amplitude: a wave.
According to the [de Broglie hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter_wave "Matter wave"), every object in the universe is associated with a [wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave "Wave"). Thus every object, from an elementary particle to atoms, molecules and on up to planets and beyond are subject to the uncertainty principle. 
The time-independent wave function of a single-moded plane wave of wavenumber _k_ 0 or momentum _p_ 0 is[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-11) ψ ( x ) ∝ e i k 0 x = e i p 0 x / ℏ . {\displaystyle \psi (x)\propto e^{ik_{0}x}=e^{ip_{0}x/\hbar }~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)\\propto e^{ik_{0}x}=e^{ip_{0}x/\\hbar }~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/28e16662c2e82f9f0d05d896c70f224b6a4f4d73)
The [Born rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Born_rule "Born rule") states that this should be interpreted as a [probability density amplitude function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probability_density_function "Probability density function") in the sense that the probability of finding the particle between _a_ and _b_ is  P ⁡ [ a ≤ X ≤ b ] = ∫ a b | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x . {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [a\leq X\leq b]=\int _{a}^{b}|\psi (x)|^{2}\,\mathrm {d} x~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[a\\leq X\\leq b\]=\\int _{a}^{b}|\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,\\mathrm {d} x~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f8852ff1f2e7c518fce8dfbb77f8e8a7920c63f3)
In the case of the single-mode plane wave,  | ψ ( x ) | 2 {\displaystyle |\psi (x)|^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/99516be05fd9132a16e5f2dcd0cb03bd632eab12) is _1_ if  X = x {\displaystyle X=x} ![{\\displaystyle X=x}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0661396d873679039ffe8e908a39f02402d4912d) and _0_ otherwise. In other words, the particle position is extremely uncertain in the sense that it could be essentially anywhere along the wave packet. 
On the other hand, consider a wave function that is a [sum of many waves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superposition_principle "Superposition principle"), which we may write as  ψ ( x ) ∝ ∑ n A n e i p n x / ℏ , {\displaystyle \psi (x)\propto \sum _{n}A_{n}e^{ip_{n}x/\hbar }~,} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)\\propto \\sum _{n}A_{n}e^{ip_{n}x/\\hbar }~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/95db00b9b9c4b8e1cc44f06f79a4fb41b2068e0f) where _A_ _n_ represents the relative contribution of the mode _p_ _n_ to the overall total. The figures to the right show how with the addition of many plane waves, the wave packet can become more localized. We may take this a step further to the [continuum limit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuum_limit "Continuum limit"), where the wave function is an [integral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integral "Integral") over all possible modes  ψ ( x ) = 1 2 π ℏ ∫ − ∞ ∞ φ ( p ) ⋅ e i p x / ℏ d p , {\displaystyle \psi (x)={\frac {1}{\sqrt {2\pi \hbar }}}\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\varphi (p)\cdot e^{ipx/\hbar }\,dp~,} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)={\\frac {1}{\\sqrt {2\\pi \\hbar }}}\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\varphi \(p\)\\cdot e^{ipx/\\hbar }\\,dp~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/71c75289039e5fbbd36b6cb0069d8120a2b2c493) with  φ ( p ) {\displaystyle \varphi (p)} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5010ee68a212e10a819fe83ef23d8e54cb18cf3) representing the amplitude of these modes and is called the wave function in [momentum space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Momentum_space "Momentum space"). In mathematical terms, we say that  φ ( p ) {\displaystyle \varphi (p)} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5010ee68a212e10a819fe83ef23d8e54cb18cf3) is the _[Fourier transform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourier_transform "Fourier transform")_ of  ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a596a1fb4130a47f6b88c66150497338bd6cbccc) and that _x_ and _p_ are [conjugate variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conjugate_variables "Conjugate variables"). Adding together all of these plane waves comes at a cost, namely the momentum has become less precise, having become a mixture of waves of many different momenta.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-L&L-12)
One way to quantify the precision of the position and momentum is the [standard deviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_deviation "Standard deviation")  _σ_. Since  | ψ ( x ) | 2 {\displaystyle |\psi (x)|^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/99516be05fd9132a16e5f2dcd0cb03bd632eab12) is a probability density function for position, we calculate its standard deviation. 
The precision of the position is improved, i.e. reduced _σ_ _x_ , by using many plane waves, thereby weakening the precision of the momentum, i.e. increased _σ_ _p_. Another way of stating this is that _σ_ _x_ and _σ_ _p_ have an [inverse relationship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inverse_relationship "Inverse relationship") or are at least bounded from below. This is the uncertainty principle, the exact limit of which is the Kennard bound. 
### Proof of the Kennard inequality using wave mechanics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: Proof of the Kennard inequality using wave mechanics")]
We are interested in the [variances](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variance "Variance") of position and momentum, defined as  σ x 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ x 2 ⋅ | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x − ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ x ⋅ | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x ) 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }x^{2}\cdot |\psi (x)|^{2}\,dx-\left(\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }x\cdot |\psi (x)|^{2}\,dx\right)^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }x^{2}\\cdot |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,dx-\\left\(\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }x\\cdot |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,dx\\right\)^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bad6520ac58c737691dbccd9da72ec5adabe39a9) σ p 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ p 2 ⋅ | φ ( p ) | 2 d p − ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ p ⋅ | φ ( p ) | 2 d p ) 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }p^{2}\cdot |\varphi (p)|^{2}\,dp-\left(\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }p\cdot |\varphi (p)|^{2}\,dp\right)^{2}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }p^{2}\\cdot |\\varphi \(p\)|^{2}\\,dp-\\left\(\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }p\\cdot |\\varphi \(p\)|^{2}\\,dp\\right\)^{2}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6fd90523039af363ab1aaa195c9717466d12384d)
[Without loss of generality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Without_loss_of_generality "Without loss of generality"), we will assume that the [means](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expected_value "Expected value") vanish, which just amounts to a shift of the origin of our coordinates. (A more general proof that does not make this assumption is given below.) This gives us the simpler form  σ x 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ x 2 ⋅ | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }x^{2}\cdot |\psi (x)|^{2}\,dx} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }x^{2}\\cdot |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,dx}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/49d3724cdf573b687867e6cc380835edeb3c6e1a) σ p 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ p 2 ⋅ | φ ( p ) | 2 d p . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }p^{2}\cdot |\varphi (p)|^{2}\,dp~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }p^{2}\\cdot |\\varphi \(p\)|^{2}\\,dp~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/968e6d598cd1b9a9714466a4767b1b7b84dcddc7)
The function  f ( x ) = x ⋅ ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle f(x)=x\cdot \psi (x)} ![{\\displaystyle f\(x\)=x\\cdot \\psi \(x\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4f0203bb7bcbeb3e324c260a3dd148b81d7db736) can be interpreted as a [vector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vector_space "Vector space") in a [function space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Function_space "Function space"). We can define an [inner product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_product "Inner product") for a pair of functions _u_(_x_) and _v_(_x_) in this vector space:  ⟨ u ∣ v ⟩ = ∫ − ∞ ∞ u ∗ ( x ) ⋅ v ( x ) d x , {\displaystyle \langle u\mid v\rangle =\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }u^{*}(x)\cdot v(x)\,dx,} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle u\\mid v\\rangle =\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }u^{*}\(x\)\\cdot v\(x\)\\,dx,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5db5a99ebfa31258e61ae2b4cce4a7813e0ea001) where the asterisk denotes the [complex conjugate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complex_conjugate "Complex conjugate"). 
With this inner product defined, we note that the variance for position can be written as  σ x 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ( x ) | 2 d x = ⟨ f ∣ f ⟩ . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|f(x)|^{2}\,dx=\langle f\mid f\rangle ~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|f\(x\)|^{2}\\,dx=\\langle f\\mid f\\rangle ~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6f4c0aa0608d33bb6696cca76da316a6c7fc456b)
We can repeat this for momentum by interpreting the function  g ~ ( p ) = p ⋅ φ ( p ) {\displaystyle {\tilde {g}}(p)=p\cdot \varphi (p)} ![{\\displaystyle {\\tilde {g}}\(p\)=p\\cdot \\varphi \(p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/81ab6c9a6e4680ef29bec94b81a9812a887b97f2) as a vector, but we can also take advantage of the fact that  ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a596a1fb4130a47f6b88c66150497338bd6cbccc) and  φ ( p ) {\displaystyle \varphi (p)} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5010ee68a212e10a819fe83ef23d8e54cb18cf3) are Fourier transforms of each other. We evaluate the inverse Fourier transform through [integration by parts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integration_by_parts "Integration by parts"):  g ( x ) = 1 2 π ℏ ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ g ~ ( p ) ⋅ e i p x / ℏ d p = 1 2 π ℏ ∫ − ∞ ∞ p ⋅ φ ( p ) ⋅ e i p x / ℏ d p = 1 2 π ℏ ∫ − ∞ ∞ [ p ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ( χ ) e − i p χ / ℏ d χ ] ⋅ e i p x / ℏ d p = i 2 π ∫ − ∞ ∞ [ ψ ( χ ) e − i p χ / ℏ | − ∞ ∞ − ∫ − ∞ ∞ d ψ ( χ ) d χ e − i p χ / ℏ d χ ] ⋅ e i p x / ℏ d p = − i ∫ − ∞ ∞ d ψ ( χ ) d χ [ 1 2 π ∫ − ∞ ∞ e i p ( x − χ ) / ℏ d p ] d χ = − i ∫ − ∞ ∞ d ψ ( χ ) d χ [ δ ( x − χ ℏ ) ] d χ = − i ℏ ∫ − ∞ ∞ d ψ ( χ ) d χ [ δ integration by parts, the cancelled term vanishes because the wave function vanishes at both infinities and  | e − i p χ / ℏ | = 1 {\displaystyle |e^{-ip\chi /\hbar }|=1} ![{\\displaystyle |e^{-ip\\chi /\\hbar }|=1}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1d8ea7be36bf8b635331d9b2fdd99ecfdf83db18), and then use the [Dirac delta function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dirac_delta_function#History "Dirac delta function") which is valid because  d ψ ( χ ) d χ {\displaystyle {\dfrac {d\psi (\chi )}{d\chi }}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\dfrac {d\\psi \(\\chi \)}{d\\chi }}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/28714dc5d275005a8fc88eec75781592e87f6290) does not depend on _p_ . 
The term  − i ℏ d d x {\textstyle -i\hbar {\frac {d}{dx}}} ![{\\textstyle -i\\hbar {\\frac {d}{dx}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/507667ec83f11f223d62ee5e128304a9a67a9268) is called the [momentum operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Momentum_operator "Momentum operator") in position space. Applying [Plancherel's theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plancherel_theorem "Plancherel theorem"), we see that the variance for momentum can be written as  σ p 2 = ∫ − ∞ ∞ | g ~ ( p ) | 2 d p = ∫ − ∞ ∞ | g ( x ) | 2 d x = ⟨ g ∣ g ⟩ . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|{\tilde {g}}(p)|^{2}\,dp=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|g(x)|^{2}\,dx=\langle g\mid g\rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|{\\tilde {g}}\(p\)|^{2}\\,dp=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|g\(x\)|^{2}\\,dx=\\langle g\\mid g\\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/947342839365ce7dbe44180c97c81bd1550fd29f)
The [Cauchy–Schwarz inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cauchy%E2%80%93Schwarz_inequality "Cauchy–Schwarz inequality") asserts that  σ x 2 σ p 2 = ⟨ f ∣ f ⟩ ⋅ ⟨ g ∣ g ⟩ ≥ | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}\sigma _{p}^{2}=\langle f\mid f\rangle \cdot \langle g\mid g\rangle \geq |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}\\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\langle f\\mid f\\rangle \\cdot \\langle g\\mid g\\rangle \\geq |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ca1580d97b4e389bbc413db1a7bbe37c77ff4d37)
The [modulus squared](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modulus_squared "Modulus squared") of any complex number _z_ can be expressed as  | z | 2 = ( Re ( z ) ) 2 + ( Im ( z ) ) 2 ≥ ( Im ( z ) ) 2 = ( z − z ∗ 2 i ) 2 . {\displaystyle |z|^{2}={\Big (}{\text{Re}}(z){\Big )}^{2}+{\Big (}{\text{Im}}(z){\Big )}^{2}\geq {\Big (}{\text{Im}}(z){\Big )}^{2}=\left({\frac {z-z^{\ast }}{2i}}\right)^{2}.} ![{\\displaystyle |z|^{2}={\\Big \(}{\\text{Re}}\(z\){\\Big \)}^{2}+{\\Big \(}{\\text{Im}}\(z\){\\Big \)}^{2}\\geq {\\Big \(}{\\text{Im}}\(z\){\\Big \)}^{2}=\\left\({\\frac {z-z^{\\ast }}{2i}}\\right\)^{2}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/03b1c5b6629593e2a0a8c37fb2f8dd1ebe8aebca) we let  z = ⟨ f | g ⟩ {\displaystyle z=\langle f|g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle z=\\langle f|g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/58b2de6fe5c8c2db6066fb583fb290fd01596e82) and  z ∗ = ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ {\displaystyle z^{*}=\langle g\mid f\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle z^{*}=\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5c66d1f8890a6d1f37b8ea4390c26763524222e) and substitute these into the equation above to get  | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 ≥ ( ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ − ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ 2 i ) 2 . {\displaystyle |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}\geq \left({\frac {\langle f\mid g\rangle -\langle g\mid f\rangle }{2i}}\right)^{2}~.} ![{\\displaystyle |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}\\geq \\left\({\\frac {\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle -\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }{2i}}\\right\)^{2}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5eea92668838e970c4a14a005bd6b860f388b6)
All that remains is to evaluate these inner products. 
⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ − ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ = ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ∗ ( x ) x ⋅ ( − i ℏ d d x ) ψ ( x ) d x − ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ∗ ( x ) ( − i ℏ d d x ) ⋅ x ψ ( x ) d x = i ℏ ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ∗ ( x ) [ ( − x ⋅ d ψ ( x ) d x ) + d ( x ψ ( x ) ) d x ] d x = i ℏ ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ∗ ( x ) [ ( − x ⋅ d ψ ( x ) d x ) + ψ ( x ) + ( x ⋅ d ψ ( x ) d x ) ] d x = i ℏ ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ ψ ∗ ( x ) ψ ( x ) d x = i ℏ ⋅ ∫ − ∞ ∞ | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x = i ℏ {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\langle f\mid g\rangle -\langle g\mid f\rangle &=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\psi ^{*}(x)\,x\cdot \left(-i\hbar {\frac {d}{dx}}\right)\,\psi (x)\,dx-\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\psi ^{*}(x)\,\left(-i\hbar {\frac {d}{dx}}\right)\cdot x\,\psi (x)\,dx\\\&=i\hbar \cdot \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\psi ^{*}(x)\left[\left(-x\cdot {\frac {d\psi (x)}{dx}}\right)+{\frac {d(x\psi (x))}{dx}}\right]\,dx\\\&=i\hbar \cdot \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\psi ^{*}(x)\left[\left(-x\cdot {\frac {d\psi (x)}{dx}}\right)+\psi (x)+\left(x\cdot {\frac {d\psi (x)}{dx}}\right)\right]\,dx\\\&=i\hbar \cdot \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\psi ^{*}(x)\psi (x)\,dx\\\&=i\hbar \cdot \int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|\psi (x)|^{2}\,dx\\\&=i\hbar \end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle -\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle &=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\psi ^{*}\(x\)\\,x\\cdot \\left\(-i\\hbar {\\frac {d}{dx}}\\right\)\\,\\psi \(x\)\\,dx-\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\psi ^{*}\(x\)\\,\\left\(-i\\hbar {\\frac {d}{dx}}\\right\)\\cdot x\\,\\psi \(x\)\\,dx\\\\&=i\\hbar \\cdot \\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\psi ^{*}\(x\)\\left\[\\left\(-x\\cdot {\\frac {d\\psi \(x\)}{dx}}\\right\)+{\\frac {d\(x\\psi \(x\)\)}{dx}}\\right\]\\,dx\\\\&=i\\hbar \\cdot \\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\psi ^{*}\(x\)\\left\[\\left\(-x\\cdot {\\frac {d\\psi \(x\)}{dx}}\\right\)+\\psi \(x\)+\\left\(x\\cdot {\\frac {d\\psi \(x\)}{dx}}\\right\)\\right\]\\,dx\\\\&=i\\hbar \\cdot \\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\psi ^{*}\(x\)\\psi \(x\)\\,dx\\\\&=i\\hbar \\cdot \\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,dx\\\\&=i\\hbar \\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8c74c70104460e61c35b8817406850728fdca3a7)
Plugging this into the above inequalities, we get  σ x 2 σ p 2 ≥ | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 ≥ ( ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ − ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ 2 i ) 2 = ( i ℏ 2 i ) 2 = ℏ 2 4 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}\sigma _{p}^{2}\geq |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}\geq \left({\frac {\langle f\mid g\rangle -\langle g\mid f\rangle }{2i}}\right)^{2}=\left({\frac {i\hbar }{2i}}\right)^{2}={\frac {\hbar ^{2}}{4}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}\\sigma _{p}^{2}\\geq |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}\\geq \\left\({\\frac {\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle -\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }{2i}}\\right\)^{2}=\\left\({\\frac {i\\hbar }{2i}}\\right\)^{2}={\\frac {\\hbar ^{2}}{4}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/766c51bffd4de26b7bc1caeb4f9835fc00d302e7) and taking the square root  σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/72455c133a439fe8679b97dc8d1629eef650d45a)
with equality if and only if _p_ and _x_ are linearly dependent. Note that the only _physics_ involved in this proof was that  ψ ( x ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a596a1fb4130a47f6b88c66150497338bd6cbccc) and  φ ( p ) {\displaystyle \varphi (p)} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5010ee68a212e10a819fe83ef23d8e54cb18cf3) are wave functions for position and momentum, which are Fourier transforms of each other. A similar result would hold for _any_ pair of conjugate variables. 
### Matrix mechanics interpretation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: Matrix mechanics interpretation")]
Main article: [Matrix mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrix_mechanics "Matrix mechanics")
In matrix mechanics, observables such as position and momentum are represented by self-adjoint operators.[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-L&L-12) When considering pairs of observables, an important quantity is the _[commutator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commutator "Commutator")_. For a pair of operators Â and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38), one defines their commutator as  [ A ^ , B ^ ] = A ^ B ^ − B ^ A ^ . {\displaystyle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]={\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}-{\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]={\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}-{\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3133da214f8f0fcf1ba11e907cfd121a2179b1b4) In the case of position and momentum, the commutator is the [canonical commutation relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonical_commutation_relation "Canonical commutation relation") [ x ^ , p ^ ] = i ℏ . {\displaystyle [{\hat {x}},{\hat {p}}]=i\hbar .} ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {x}},{\\hat {p}}\]=i\\hbar .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fee0861ae7784cb51a1b43f6c51735c22c23274e)
The physical meaning of the non-commutativity can be understood by considering the effect of the commutator on position and momentum [eigenstates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eigenstate "Eigenstate"). Let  | ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc27f1893b769a08cd6b296e115a29e61cab675e) be a right eigenstate of position with a constant eigenvalue _x_ 0. By definition, this means that  x ^ | ψ ⟩ = x 0 | ψ ⟩ . {\displaystyle {\hat {x}}|\psi \rangle =x_{0}|\psi \rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {x}}|\\psi \\rangle =x_{0}|\\psi \\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/99974c4e6ad4d675c7552a4228f51c68b184349a) Applying the commutator to  | ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc27f1893b769a08cd6b296e115a29e61cab675e) yields  [ x ^ , p ^ ] | ψ ⟩ = ( x ^ p ^ − p ^ x ^ ) | ψ ⟩ = ( x ^ − x 0 I ^ ) p ^ | ψ ⟩ = i ℏ | ψ ⟩ , {\displaystyle [{\hat {x}},{\hat {p}}]|\psi \rangle =({\hat {x}}{\hat {p}}-{\hat {p}}{\hat {x}})|\psi \rangle =({\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\hat {I}}){\hat {p}}\,|\psi \rangle =i\hbar |\psi \rangle ,} ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {x}},{\\hat {p}}\]|\\psi \\rangle =\({\\hat {x}}{\\hat {p}}-{\\hat {p}}{\\hat {x}}\)|\\psi \\rangle =\({\\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\\hat {I}}\){\\hat {p}}\\,|\\psi \\rangle =i\\hbar |\\psi \\rangle ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8255257353e4743e966c106dea5674689ed04690) where Î is the [identity operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Identity_matrix "Identity matrix"). 
Suppose, for the sake of [proof by contradiction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proof_by_contradiction "Proof by contradiction"), that  | ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc27f1893b769a08cd6b296e115a29e61cab675e) is also a right eigenstate of momentum, with constant eigenvalue _p_ 0. If this were true, then one could write  ( x ^ − x 0 I ^ ) p ^ | ψ ⟩ = ( x ^ − x 0 I ^ ) p 0 | ψ ⟩ = ( x 0 I ^ − x 0 I ^ ) p 0 | ψ ⟩ = 0. {\displaystyle ({\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\hat {I}}){\hat {p}}\,|\psi \rangle =({\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\hat {I}})p_{0}\,|\psi \rangle =(x_{0}{\hat {I}}-x_{0}{\hat {I}})p_{0}\,|\psi \rangle =0.} ![{\\displaystyle \({\\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\\hat {I}}\){\\hat {p}}\\,|\\psi \\rangle =\({\\hat {x}}-x_{0}{\\hat {I}}\)p_{0}\\,|\\psi \\rangle =\(x_{0}{\\hat {I}}-x_{0}{\\hat {I}}\)p_{0}\\,|\\psi \\rangle =0.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/49ffc3dad1441f176ffe0df7d6d3001872c9d497) On the other hand, the above canonical commutation relation requires that  [ x ^ , p ^ ] | ψ ⟩ = i ℏ | ψ ⟩ ≠ 0. {\displaystyle [{\hat {x}},{\hat {p}}]|\psi \rangle =i\hbar |\psi \rangle \neq 0.} ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {x}},{\\hat {p}}\]|\\psi \\rangle =i\\hbar |\\psi \\rangle \\neq 0.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/04d341bbd1c29d8f96a5ce5ad6e2cf856d1b1f40) This implies that no quantum state can simultaneously be both a position and a momentum eigenstate. 
When a state is measured, it is projected onto an eigenstate in the basis of the relevant observable. For example, if a particle's position is measured, then the state amounts to a position eigenstate. This means that the state is _not_ a momentum eigenstate, however, but rather it can be represented as a sum of multiple momentum basis eigenstates. In other words, the momentum must be less precise. This precision may be quantified by the standard deviations,  σ x = ⟨ x ^ 2 ⟩ − ⟨ x ^ ⟩ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}={\sqrt {\langle {\hat {x}}^{2}\rangle -\langle {\hat {x}}\rangle ^{2}}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}={\\sqrt {\\langle {\\hat {x}}^{2}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {x}}\\rangle ^{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cb2f8b00d0a80409413ea7591c2a4d6afcb5f0f0) σ p = ⟨ p ^ 2 ⟩ − ⟨ p ^ ⟩ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}={\sqrt {\langle {\hat {p}}^{2}\rangle -\langle {\hat {p}}\rangle ^{2}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}={\\sqrt {\\langle {\\hat {p}}^{2}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {p}}\\rangle ^{2}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f734a04ab5323ebefafbef5d797d35afc2773fab)
As in the wave mechanics interpretation above, one sees a tradeoff between the respective precisions of the two, quantified by the uncertainty principle. 
### Quantum harmonic oscillator stationary states
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Quantum harmonic oscillator stationary states")]
Main articles: [Quantum harmonic oscillator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_harmonic_oscillator "Quantum harmonic oscillator") and [Stationary state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stationary_state "Stationary state")
Consider a one-dimensional [quantum harmonic oscillator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_harmonic_oscillator "Quantum harmonic oscillator"). It is possible to express the position and momentum operators in terms of the [creation and annihilation operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Creation_and_annihilation_operators "Creation and annihilation operators"):  x ^ = ℏ 2 m ω ( a + a † ) {\displaystyle {\hat {x}}={\sqrt {\frac {\hbar }{2m\omega }}}(a+a^{\dagger })} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {x}}={\\sqrt {\\frac {\\hbar }{2m\\omega }}}\(a+a^{\\dagger }\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f0df220fd111fa795fe792be841d09d13b3d1370) p ^ = i m ω ℏ 2 ( a † − a ) . {\displaystyle {\hat {p}}=i{\sqrt {\frac {m\omega \hbar }{2}}}(a^{\dagger }-a).} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {p}}=i{\\sqrt {\\frac {m\\omega \\hbar }{2}}}\(a^{\\dagger }-a\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7eff61f9ea51f7dcd782afadd446ae754aa927a8)
Using the standard rules for creation and annihilation operators on the energy eigenstates,  a † | n ⟩ = n + 1 | n + 1 ⟩ {\displaystyle a^{\dagger }|n\rangle ={\sqrt {n+1}}|n+1\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle a^{\\dagger }|n\\rangle ={\\sqrt {n+1}}|n+1\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d4f1a279847912c0b3fce6e3ea167e0fdf2c8af6) a | n ⟩ = n | n − 1 ⟩ , {\displaystyle a|n\rangle ={\sqrt {n}}|n-1\rangle ,} ![{\\displaystyle a|n\\rangle ={\\sqrt {n}}|n-1\\rangle ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/826e5d4bf44905dbb204a098c27da8b51ae0ed4f) the variances may be computed directly,  σ x 2 = ℏ m ω ( n + 1 2 ) {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}={\frac {\hbar }{m\omega }}\left(n+{\frac {1}{2}}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}={\\frac {\\hbar }{m\\omega }}\\left\(n+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eb151dac9eee44a14bf6c8a5783049312f85b075) σ p 2 = ℏ m ω ( n + 1 2 ) . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}=\hbar m\omega \left(n+{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\,.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\hbar m\\omega \\left\(n+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\,.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e1b6facd1a9fb3fe1799fb946d8791f04959b7b2) The product of these standard deviations is then  σ x σ p = ℏ ( n + 1 2 ) ≥ ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}=\hbar \left(n+{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}.~} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}=\\hbar \\left\(n+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.~}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2826b52afe988b79570d538ba6be527a44db9707)
In particular, the above Kennard bound[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Kennard-6) is saturated for the [ground state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_state "Ground state") _n_ =0, for which the probability density is just the [normal distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normal_distribution "Normal distribution"). 
### Quantum harmonic oscillators with Gaussian initial condition
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Quantum harmonic oscillators with Gaussian initial condition")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/f/fe/Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO%2C_balanced.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO,_balanced.gif)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/5c/Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO%2C_narrow.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO,_narrow.gif)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/68/Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO%2C_wide.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Position_and_momentum_of_a_Gaussian_initial_state_for_a_QHO,_wide.gif)
Position (blue) and momentum (red) probability densities for an initial Gaussian distribution. From top to bottom, the animations show the cases Ω = _ω_ , Ω = 2 _ω_ , and Ω = _ω_ /2. Note the tradeoff between the widths of the distributions.
In a quantum harmonic oscillator of characteristic angular frequency _ω_ , place a state that is offset from the bottom of the potential by some displacement _x_ 0 as  ψ ( x ) = ( m Ω π ℏ ) 1 / 4 exp ⁡ ( − m Ω ( x − x 0 ) 2 2 ℏ ) , {\displaystyle \psi (x)=\left({\frac {m\Omega }{\pi \hbar }}\right)^{1/4}\exp {\left(-{\frac {m\Omega (x-x_{0})^{2}}{2\hbar }}\right)},} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)=\\left\({\\frac {m\\Omega }{\\pi \\hbar }}\\right\)^{1/4}\\exp {\\left\(-{\\frac {m\\Omega \(x-x_{0}\)^{2}}{2\\hbar }}\\right\)},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0b2c7856978737646e8b07e472fa3f9480122eef) where Ω describes the width of the initial state but need not be the same as _ω_. Through integration over the [propagator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propagator#Basic_examples:_propagator_of_free_particle_and_harmonic_oscillator "Propagator"), we can solve for the full time-dependent solution. After many cancelations, the probability densities reduce to  | Ψ ( x , t ) | 2 ∼ N ( x 0 cos ⁡ ( ω t ) , ℏ 2 m Ω ( cos 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) + Ω 2 ω 2 sin 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) ) ) {\displaystyle |\Psi (x,t)|^{2}\sim {\mathcal {N}}\left(x_{0}\cos {(\omega t)},{\frac {\hbar }{2m\Omega }}\left(\cos ^{2}(\omega t)+{\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}\sin ^{2}{(\omega t)}\right)\right)} ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \(x,t\)|^{2}\\sim {\\mathcal {N}}\\left\(x_{0}\\cos {\(\\omega t\)},{\\frac {\\hbar }{2m\\Omega }}\\left\(\\cos ^{2}\(\\omega t\)+{\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}\\sin ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}\\right\)\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/709bcb325511662282b2e2c2388aec900d57a65c) | Φ ( p , t ) | 2 ∼ N ( − m x 0 ω sin ⁡ ( ω t ) , ℏ m Ω 2 ( cos 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) + ω 2 Ω 2 sin 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) ) ) , {\displaystyle |\Phi (p,t)|^{2}\sim {\mathcal {N}}\left(-mx_{0}\omega \sin(\omega t),{\frac {\hbar m\Omega }{2}}\left(\cos ^{2}{(\omega t)}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\sin ^{2}{(\omega t)}\right)\right),} ![{\\displaystyle |\\Phi \(p,t\)|^{2}\\sim {\\mathcal {N}}\\left\(-mx_{0}\\omega \\sin\(\\omega t\),{\\frac {\\hbar m\\Omega }{2}}\\left\(\\cos ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\sin ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}\\right\)\\right\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bbe945fd4f67a9b894c7399bfcc48f585c7d4dd2) where we have used the notation  N ( μ , σ 2 ) {\displaystyle {\mathcal {N}}(\mu ,\sigma ^{2})} ![{\\displaystyle {\\mathcal {N}}\(\\mu ,\\sigma ^{2}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/863304aaa42a945f2f07d79facc3d2eebc845ce7) to denote a normal distribution of mean _μ_ and variance _σ_ 2. Copying the variances above and applying [trigonometric identities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_trigonometric_identities "List of trigonometric identities"), we can write the product of the standard deviations as  σ x σ p = ℏ 2 ( cos 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) + Ω 2 ω 2 sin 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) ) ( cos 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) + ω 2 Ω 2 sin 2 ⁡ ( ω t ) ) = ℏ 4 3 + 1 2 ( Ω 2 ω 2 + ω 2 Ω 2 ) − ( 1 2 ( Ω 2 ω 2 + ω 2 Ω 2 ) − 1 ) cos ⁡ ( 4 ω t ) {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}&={\frac {\hbar }{2}}{\sqrt {\left(\cos ^{2}{(\omega t)}+{\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}\sin ^{2}{(\omega t)}\right)\left(\cos ^{2}{(\omega t)}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\sin ^{2}{(\omega t)}\right)}}\\\&={\frac {\hbar }{4}}{\sqrt {3+{\frac {1}{2}}\left({\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\right)-\left({\frac {1}{2}}\left({\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\right)-1\right)\cos {(4\omega t)}}}\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}\\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}&={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}{\\sqrt {\\left\(\\cos ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}+{\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}\\sin ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}\\right\)\\left\(\\cos ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\sin ^{2}{\(\\omega t\)}\\right\)}}\\\\&={\\frac {\\hbar }{4}}{\\sqrt {3+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\({\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\right\)-\\left\({\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\({\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\right\)-1\\right\)\\cos {\(4\\omega t\)}}}\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/21b7f83efceda3c3069130ab37f23695fea2d013)
From the relations  Ω 2 ω 2 + ω 2 Ω 2 ≥ 2 , | cos ⁡ ( 4 ω t ) | ≤ 1 , {\displaystyle {\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\geq 2,\quad |\cos(4\omega t)|\leq 1,} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\geq 2,\\quad |\\cos\(4\\omega t\)|\\leq 1,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45abbc3dc26e9cb7cd7029727fac07fdc0f8f13d) we can conclude the following (the right most equality holds only when Ω = _ω_):  σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 4 3 + 1 2 ( Ω 2 ω 2 + ω 2 Ω 2 ) − ( 1 2 ( Ω 2 ω 2 + ω 2 Ω 2 ) − 1 ) = ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{4}}{\sqrt {3+{\frac {1}{2}}\left({\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\right)-\left({\frac {1}{2}}\left({\frac {\Omega ^{2}}{\omega ^{2}}}+{\frac {\omega ^{2}}{\Omega ^{2}}}\right)-1\right)}}={\frac {\hbar }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{4}}{\\sqrt {3+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\({\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\right\)-\\left\({\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\({\\frac {\\Omega ^{2}}{\\omega ^{2}}}+{\\frac {\\omega ^{2}}{\\Omega ^{2}}}\\right\)-1\\right\)}}={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/262252286a0dedb9523f929950017c05e2a943a7)
### Coherent states
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Coherent states")]
Main article: [Coherent state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coherent_state "Coherent state")
A coherent state is a right eigenstate of the [annihilation operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annihilation_operator "Annihilation operator"),  a ^ | α ⟩ = α | α ⟩ , {\displaystyle {\hat {a}}|\alpha \rangle =\alpha |\alpha \rangle ,} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {a}}|\\alpha \\rangle =\\alpha |\\alpha \\rangle ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/009e84c11190047e83348b026493c84c8957f3a1) which may be represented in terms of [Fock states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fock_state "Fock state") as  | α ⟩ = e − | α | 2 2 ∑ n = 0 ∞ α n n ! | n ⟩ {\displaystyle |\alpha \rangle =e^{-{|\alpha |^{2} \over 2}}\sum _{n=0}^{\infty }{\alpha ^{n} \over {\sqrt {n!}}}|n\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\alpha \\rangle =e^{-{|\\alpha |^{2} \\over 2}}\\sum _{n=0}^{\\infty }{\\alpha ^{n} \\over {\\sqrt {n!}}}|n\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c8cf358f5edec1a340fc53e370658f93d846a237)
In the picture where the coherent state is a massive particle in a quantum harmonic oscillator, the position and momentum operators may be expressed in terms of the annihilation operators in the same formulas above and used to calculate the variances,  σ x 2 = ℏ 2 m ω , {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}={\frac {\hbar }{2m\omega }},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}={\\frac {\\hbar }{2m\\omega }},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a3785eb7c2472c595db5e2e00f9a308e1f282d92) σ p 2 = ℏ m ω 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}={\frac {\hbar m\omega }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}={\\frac {\\hbar m\\omega }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6b3366470e3a9809822895d0e460f23e322c6f4d) Therefore, every coherent state saturates the Kennard bound  σ x σ p = ℏ 2 m ω ℏ m ω 2 = ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}={\sqrt {\frac {\hbar }{2m\omega }}}\,{\sqrt {\frac {\hbar m\omega }{2}}}={\frac {\hbar }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}={\\sqrt {\\frac {\\hbar }{2m\\omega }}}\\,{\\sqrt {\\frac {\\hbar m\\omega }{2}}}={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9c6ce20c15575b2df5731080b3c6925acae2ad1b) with position and momentum each contributing an amount  ℏ / 2 {\textstyle {\sqrt {\hbar /2}}} ![{\\textstyle {\\sqrt {\\hbar /2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/24016eff3857464a91cb2e8407a9cda457a72408) in a "balanced" way. Moreover, every [squeezed coherent state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squeezed_coherent_state "Squeezed coherent state") also saturates the Kennard bound although the individual contributions of position and momentum need not be balanced in general. 
### Particle in a box
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Particle in a box")]
Main article: [Particle in a box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_in_a_box "Particle in a box")
Consider a particle in a one-dimensional box of length  L {\displaystyle L} ![{\\displaystyle L}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/103168b86f781fe6e9a4a87b8ea1cebe0ad4ede8). The [eigenfunctions in position and momentum space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_in_a_box#Wavefunctions "Particle in a box") are  ψ n ( x , t ) = { A sin ⁡ ( k n x ) e − i ω n t , 0 < x < L , 0 , otherwise, {\displaystyle \psi _{n}(x,t)={\begin{cases}A\sin(k_{n}x)\mathrm {e} ^{-\mathrm {i} \omega _{n}t},&0<x<L,\\\0,&{\text{otherwise,}}\end{cases}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi _{n}\(x,t\)={\\begin{cases}A\\sin\(k_{n}x\)\\mathrm {e} ^{-\\mathrm {i} \\omega _{n}t},&0<x<L,\\\\0,&{\\text{otherwise,}}\\end{cases}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c94257ba27fc7b6ddb253e5f132e5cb94fee43b8) and  φ n ( p , t ) = π L ℏ n ( 1 − ( − 1 ) n e − i k L ) e − i ω n t π 2 n 2 − k 2 L 2 , {\displaystyle \varphi _{n}(p,t)={\sqrt {\frac {\pi L}{\hbar }}}\,\,{\frac {n\left(1-(-1)^{n}e^{-ikL}\right)e^{-i\omega _{n}t}}{\pi ^{2}n^{2}-k^{2}L^{2}}},} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi _{n}\(p,t\)={\\sqrt {\\frac {\\pi L}{\\hbar }}}\\,\\,{\\frac {n\\left\(1-\(-1\)^{n}e^{-ikL}\\right\)e^{-i\\omega _{n}t}}{\\pi ^{2}n^{2}-k^{2}L^{2}}},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7197979de2bf9f6e472fa0c6288f54e1a5346f50) where  ω n = π 2 ℏ n 2 8 L 2 m {\textstyle \omega _{n}={\frac {\pi ^{2}\hbar n^{2}}{8L^{2}m}}} ![{\\textstyle \\omega _{n}={\\frac {\\pi ^{2}\\hbar n^{2}}{8L^{2}m}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/79069e7d7f481765db92bd8fa32af17d29f97158) and we have used the [de Broglie relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Broglie_relation "De Broglie relation") p = ℏ k {\displaystyle p=\hbar k} ![{\\displaystyle p=\\hbar k}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/24fee69175538303b28ac54e907baf53d0a58dbf). The variances of  x {\displaystyle x} ![{\\displaystyle x}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/87f9e315fd7e2ba406057a97300593c4802b53e4) and  p {\displaystyle p} ![{\\displaystyle p}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/81eac1e205430d1f40810df36a0edffdc367af36) can be calculated explicitly:  σ x 2 = L 2 12 ( 1 − 6 n 2 π 2 ) {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}={\frac {L^{2}}{12}}\left(1-{\frac {6}{n^{2}\pi ^{2}}}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}={\\frac {L^{2}}{12}}\\left\(1-{\\frac {6}{n^{2}\\pi ^{2}}}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cd6a2e8bfd65babe2bea2ea66906856f1ca26db7) σ p 2 = ( ℏ n π L ) 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}^{2}=\left({\frac {\hbar n\pi }{L}}\right)^{2}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\left\({\\frac {\\hbar n\\pi }{L}}\\right\)^{2}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/62b2370fe42c65d0b79e93ddb640a5d629140eac)
The product of the standard deviations is therefore  σ x σ p = ℏ 2 n 2 π 2 3 − 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}={\frac {\hbar }{2}}{\sqrt {{\frac {n^{2}\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}{\\sqrt {{\\frac {n^{2}\\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7b7051cdf63a3e433817b98489d4c5333bbc19a4) For all  n = 1 , 2 , 3 , … {\displaystyle n=1,\,2,\,3,\,\ldots } ![{\\displaystyle n=1,\\,2,\\,3,\\,\\ldots }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d7aea7d9828557e2300f79018dcf2f879fa47ab6), the quantity  n 2 π 2 3 − 2 {\textstyle {\sqrt {{\frac {n^{2}\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}} ![{\\textstyle {\\sqrt {{\\frac {n^{2}\\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ee11348f995d08f8328d34fb8f99e9d6f9eb3ef5) is greater than 1, so the uncertainty principle is never violated. For numerical concreteness, the smallest value occurs when  n = 1 {\displaystyle n=1} ![{\\displaystyle n=1}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d9ec7e1edc2e6d98f5aec2a39ae5f1c99d1e1425), in which case  σ x σ p = ℏ 2 π 2 3 − 2 ≈ 0.568 ℏ > ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}={\frac {\hbar }{2}}{\sqrt {{\frac {\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}\approx 0.568\hbar >{\frac {\hbar }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}{\\sqrt {{\\frac {\\pi ^{2}}{3}}-2}}\\approx 0.568\\hbar >{\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/edd3a568ee8802277749daa4043f19f2c4aab3e4)
### Constant momentum
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Constant momentum")]
Main article: [Wave packet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_packet "Wave packet")
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/5/56/Guassian_Dispersion.gif)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Guassian_Dispersion.gif)Position space probability density of an initially Gaussian state moving at minimally uncertain, constant momentum in free space
Assume a particle initially has a [momentum space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Momentum_space "Momentum space") wave function described by a normal distribution around some constant momentum _p_ 0 according to  φ ( p ) = ( x 0 ℏ π ) 1 / 2 exp ⁡ ( − x 0 2 ( p − p 0 ) 2 2 ℏ 2 ) , {\displaystyle \varphi (p)=\left({\frac {x_{0}}{\hbar {\sqrt {\pi }}}}\right)^{1/2}\exp \left({\frac {-x_{0}^{2}(p-p_{0})^{2}}{2\hbar ^{2}}}\right),} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)=\\left\({\\frac {x_{0}}{\\hbar {\\sqrt {\\pi }}}}\\right\)^{1/2}\\exp \\left\({\\frac {-x_{0}^{2}\(p-p_{0}\)^{2}}{2\\hbar ^{2}}}\\right\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/86b0f293c800fb9e6ab70f59020d1037cba300a0) where we have introduced a reference scale  x 0 = ℏ / m ω 0 {\textstyle x_{0}={\sqrt {\hbar /m\omega _{0}}}} ![{\\textstyle x_{0}={\\sqrt {\\hbar /m\\omega _{0}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c8dd8d3364154b4817d4295aa871a7d26d665432), with  ω 0 > 0 {\displaystyle \omega _{0}>0} ![{\\displaystyle \\omega _{0}>0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b1760f9faf283234d7341a658b0431cde4510c66) describing the width of the distribution—cf. [nondimensionalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondimensionalization "Nondimensionalization"). If the state is allowed to evolve in free space, then the time-dependent momentum and position space wave functions are  Φ ( p , t ) = ( x 0 ℏ π ) 1 / 2 exp ⁡ ( − x 0 2 ( p − p 0 ) 2 2 ℏ 2 − i p 2 t 2 m ℏ ) , {\displaystyle \Phi (p,t)=\left({\frac {x_{0}}{\hbar {\sqrt {\pi }}}}\right)^{1/2}\exp \left({\frac {-x_{0}^{2}(p-p_{0})^{2}}{2\hbar ^{2}}}-{\frac {ip^{2}t}{2m\hbar }}\right),} ![{\\displaystyle \\Phi \(p,t\)=\\left\({\\frac {x_{0}}{\\hbar {\\sqrt {\\pi }}}}\\right\)^{1/2}\\exp \\left\({\\frac {-x_{0}^{2}\(p-p_{0}\)^{2}}{2\\hbar ^{2}}}-{\\frac {ip^{2}t}{2m\\hbar }}\\right\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ee785920dd03421fa259a8735f6299c6d428fef1) Ψ ( x , t ) = ( 1 x 0 π ) 1 / 2 e − x 0 2 p 0 2 / 2 ℏ 2 1 + i ω 0 t exp ⁡ ( − ( x − i x 0 2 p 0 / ℏ ) 2 2 x 0 2 ( 1 + i ω 0 t ) ) . {\displaystyle \Psi (x,t)=\left({\frac {1}{x_{0}{\sqrt {\pi }}}}\right)^{1/2}{\frac {e^{-x_{0}^{2}p_{0}^{2}/2\hbar ^{2}}}{\sqrt {1+i\omega _{0}t}}}\,\exp \left(-{\frac {(x-ix_{0}^{2}p_{0}/\hbar )^{2}}{2x_{0}^{2}(1+i\omega _{0}t)}}\right).} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \(x,t\)=\\left\({\\frac {1}{x_{0}{\\sqrt {\\pi }}}}\\right\)^{1/2}{\\frac {e^{-x_{0}^{2}p_{0}^{2}/2\\hbar ^{2}}}{\\sqrt {1+i\\omega _{0}t}}}\\,\\exp \\left\(-{\\frac {\(x-ix_{0}^{2}p_{0}/\\hbar \)^{2}}{2x_{0}^{2}\(1+i\\omega _{0}t\)}}\\right\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6b656285207c038d8c3385f80cec2c32aa677a56)
Since  ⟨ p ( t ) ⟩ = p 0 {\displaystyle \langle p(t)\rangle =p_{0}} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle p\(t\)\\rangle =p_{0}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/028662693d4c01af288d92fb6da3a9f0eafc43b1) and  σ p ( t ) = ℏ / ( 2 x 0 ) {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}(t)=\hbar /({\sqrt {2}}x_{0})} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}\(t\)=\\hbar /\({\\sqrt {2}}x_{0}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e11952c0d139ed9b793f55e1b53b3ad35897b452), this can be interpreted as a particle moving along with constant momentum at arbitrarily high precision. On the other hand, the standard deviation of the position is  σ x = x 0 2 1 + ω 0 2 t 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}={\frac {x_{0}}{\sqrt {2}}}{\sqrt {1+\omega _{0}^{2}t^{2}}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}={\\frac {x_{0}}{\\sqrt {2}}}{\\sqrt {1+\\omega _{0}^{2}t^{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/400dd0a32c7fe639d7484ef94c98833349c4b9aa) such that the uncertainty product can only increase with time as  σ x ( t ) σ p ( t ) = ℏ 2 1 + ω 0 2 t 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}(t)\sigma _{p}(t)={\frac {\hbar }{2}}{\sqrt {1+\omega _{0}^{2}t^{2}}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\(t\)\\sigma _{p}\(t\)={\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}{\\sqrt {1+\\omega _{0}^{2}t^{2}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f1e31d5785dde1968a75dd44c1fd3578c1c0cb51)
## Mathematical formalism
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Mathematical formalism")]
Starting with Kennard's derivation of position-momentum uncertainty, [Howard Percy Robertson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Percy_Robertson "Howard Percy Robertson") developed[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Robertson1929-13)[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1) a formulation for arbitrary [Hermitian operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-adjoint_operator "Self-adjoint operator") O ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {\mathcal {O}}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\mathcal {O}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fdc75171cfac6f56675cff45764791db428a97de) expressed in terms of their standard deviation  σ O = ⟨ O ^ 2 ⟩ − ⟨ O ^ ⟩ 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{\mathcal {O}}={\sqrt {\langle {\hat {\mathcal {O}}}^{2}\rangle -\langle {\hat {\mathcal {O}}}\rangle ^{2}}},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{\\mathcal {O}}={\\sqrt {\\langle {\\hat {\\mathcal {O}}}^{2}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {\\mathcal {O}}}\\rangle ^{2}}},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/691c3bc35db4376b8b2aec24936e1ef655db0f57) where the brackets  ⟨ O ^ ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle {\hat {\mathcal {O}}}\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle {\\hat {\\mathcal {O}}}\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e1d94748396e9155b941e8a6cde4e11e82d7ee5e) indicate an [expectation value](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expectation_value_\(quantum_mechanics\) "Expectation value \(quantum mechanics\)") of the observable represented by operator  O ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {\mathcal {O}}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {\\mathcal {O}}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fdc75171cfac6f56675cff45764791db428a97de). For a pair of operators  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38), define their commutator as  [ A ^ , B ^ ] = A ^ B ^ − B ^ A ^ , {\displaystyle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]={\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}-{\hat {B}}{\hat {A}},} ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]={\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}-{\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9814a77ea436e956fd7abaad7b5906457037b565) and the Robertson uncertainty relation is given by[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-14) σ A σ B ≥ | 1 2 i ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | = 1 2 | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}\sigma _{B}\geq \left|{\frac {1}{2i}}\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle \right|={\frac {1}{2}}\left|\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle \right|.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}\\sigma _{B}\\geq \\left|{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle \\right|={\\frac {1}{2}}\\left|\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle \\right|.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c4af99d41ac501f569df0076776c5fc7199a2d1a)
[Erwin Schrödinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Schr%C3%B6dinger "Erwin Schrödinger")[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-15) showed how to allow for correlation between the operators, giving a stronger inequality, known as the **Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty relation** ,[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Schrodinger1930-16)[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1)
σ A 2 σ B 2 ≥ | 1 2 ⟨ { A ^ , B ^ } ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ | 2 + | 1 2 i ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq \left|{\frac {1}{2}}\langle \\{{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}\\}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle \right|^{2}+\left|{\frac {1}{2i}}\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle \right|^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq \\left|{\\frac {1}{2}}\\langle \\{{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\\}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \\right|^{2}+\\left|{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle \\right|^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/81d7a893fe43757553fb6a3e5a51cdb83290aadd)
where the anticommutator,  { A ^ , B ^ } = A ^ B ^ + B ^ A ^ {\displaystyle \\{{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}\\}={\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}+{\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\{{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\\}={\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}+{\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/db1ed715333f79eb13ea507d5a939871030d2d02) is used. 
**Proof of the[Schrödinger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erwin_Schr%C3%B6dinger "Erwin Schrödinger") uncertainty relation**
The derivation shown here incorporates and builds off of those shown in Robertson,[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Robertson1929-13) Schrödinger[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Schrodinger1930-16) and standard textbooks such as Griffiths.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GriffithsSchroeter2018-17): 138  For any Hermitian operator  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821), based upon the definition of [variance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variance "Variance"), we have  σ A 2 = ⟨ ( A ^ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ) Ψ | ( A ^ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}=\langle ({\hat {A}}-\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle )\Psi |({\hat {A}}-\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}=\\langle \({\\hat {A}}-\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \)\\Psi |\({\\hat {A}}-\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9b0599535cffd5911b7bca99b1e04e7af5a4f095) we let  | f ⟩ = | ( A ^ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |f\rangle =|({\hat {A}}-\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |f\\rangle =|\({\\hat {A}}-\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ea80da4f3540353cffcbf5898d5bf97ca4e204b4) and thus  σ A 2 = ⟨ f ∣ f ⟩ . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}=\langle f\mid f\rangle \,.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}=\\langle f\\mid f\\rangle \\,.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/41fa95aad96f9c65039a78973b06969b9b62e486)
Similarly, for any other Hermitian operator  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) in the same state  σ B 2 = ⟨ ( B ^ − ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ | ( B ^ − ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ = ⟨ g ∣ g ⟩ {\displaystyle \sigma _{B}^{2}=\langle ({\hat {B}}-\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi |({\hat {B}}-\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle =\langle g\mid g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{B}^{2}=\\langle \({\\hat {B}}-\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi |\({\\hat {B}}-\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle =\\langle g\\mid g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/90c76538699b46f13bea1e14a0b75eff2d12a0f8) for  | g ⟩ = | ( B ^ − ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ . {\displaystyle |g\rangle =|({\hat {B}}-\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle |g\\rangle =|\({\\hat {B}}-\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b8f5c185159853c4c945061f1dce3f3b01ffb631)
The product of the two deviations can thus be expressed as 
σ A 2 σ B 2 = ⟨ f ∣ f ⟩ ⟨ g ∣ g ⟩ . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}\sigma _{B}^{2}=\langle f\mid f\rangle \langle g\mid g\rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}\\sigma _{B}^{2}=\\langle f\\mid f\\rangle \\langle g\\mid g\\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/258d7cbd5c4569b2b8bb9cb29300fa65f386d1af) |  | 1  
---|---|---  
In order to relate the two vectors  | f ⟩ {\displaystyle |f\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |f\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7d19bc13e285667d4f27ab4fc8be7327ad833294) and  | g ⟩ {\displaystyle |g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3453f56954453a2c243cdce6e6b246d9b6c578fc), we use the [Cauchy–Schwarz inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cauchy%E2%80%93Schwarz_inequality "Cauchy–Schwarz inequality")[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Riley2006-18) which is defined as  ⟨ f ∣ f ⟩ ⟨ g ∣ g ⟩ ≥ | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 , {\displaystyle \langle f\mid f\rangle \langle g\mid g\rangle \geq |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid f\\rangle \\langle g\\mid g\\rangle \\geq |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a24abb83f05e1c07a50af279e86a0755e60920ca) and thus Equation (**[1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#math_1)**) can be written as 
σ A 2 σ B 2 ≥ | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5b3aa4b94d37b7dd5d8a9dc84432f693815b5ea4) |  | 2  
---|---|---  
Since  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle f\mid g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ef1052f379f4185222d9c628cd2cab8503ef7d3b) is in general a complex number, we use the fact that the modulus squared of any complex number  z {\displaystyle z} ![{\\displaystyle z}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bf368e72c009decd9b6686ee84a375632e11de98) is defined as  | z | 2 = z z ∗ {\displaystyle |z|^{2}=zz^{*}} ![{\\displaystyle |z|^{2}=zz^{*}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4856c7e3f00087230f8c7128f4d4a9343df5e894), where  z ∗ {\displaystyle z^{*}} ![{\\displaystyle z^{*}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/a5b376dccffe5ae946dcdb7e98bf41beae28dc9e) is the complex conjugate of  z {\displaystyle z} ![{\\displaystyle z}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bf368e72c009decd9b6686ee84a375632e11de98). The modulus squared can also be expressed as 
| z | 2 = ( Re ⁡ ( z ) ) 2 + ( Im ⁡ ( z ) ) 2 = ( z + z ∗ 2 ) 2 + ( z − z ∗ 2 i ) 2 . {\displaystyle |z|^{2}={\Big (}\operatorname {Re} (z){\Big )}^{2}+{\Big (}\operatorname {Im} (z){\Big )}^{2}={\Big (}{\frac {z+z^{\ast }}{2}}{\Big )}^{2}+{\Big (}{\frac {z-z^{\ast }}{2i}}{\Big )}^{2}.} ![{\\displaystyle |z|^{2}={\\Big \(}\\operatorname {Re} \(z\){\\Big \)}^{2}+{\\Big \(}\\operatorname {Im} \(z\){\\Big \)}^{2}={\\Big \(}{\\frac {z+z^{\\ast }}{2}}{\\Big \)}^{2}+{\\Big \(}{\\frac {z-z^{\\ast }}{2i}}{\\Big \)}^{2}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/26dd6ce4e5639bda5f698896277f2082b3de09a8) |  | 3  
---|---|---  
we let  z = ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ {\displaystyle z=\langle f\mid g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle z=\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/33e2ee0ada46b654be6470c5e3f6f9a0b07acf0b) and  z ∗ = ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ {\displaystyle z^{*}=\langle g\mid f\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle z^{*}=\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e5c66d1f8890a6d1f37b8ea4390c26763524222e) and substitute these into the equation above to get 
| ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 = ( ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ + ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ 2 ) 2 + ( ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ − ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ 2 i ) 2 {\displaystyle |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}={\bigg (}{\frac {\langle f\mid g\rangle +\langle g\mid f\rangle }{2}}{\bigg )}^{2}+{\bigg (}{\frac {\langle f\mid g\rangle -\langle g\mid f\rangle }{2i}}{\bigg )}^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}={\\bigg \(}{\\frac {\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle +\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }{2}}{\\bigg \)}^{2}+{\\bigg \(}{\\frac {\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle -\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle }{2i}}{\\bigg \)}^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/762f400374ef32591ff1d098a34c5740da5a5141) |  | 4  
---|---|---  
The inner product  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle f\mid g\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid g\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ef1052f379f4185222d9c628cd2cab8503ef7d3b) is written out explicitly as  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ = ⟨ ( A ^ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ) Ψ | ( B ^ − ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ , {\displaystyle \langle f\mid g\rangle =\langle ({\hat {A}}-\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle )\Psi |({\hat {B}}-\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle ,} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid g\\rangle =\\langle \({\\hat {A}}-\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \)\\Psi |\({\\hat {B}}-\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e797c8d6e151a04287d33c28a61a411021e6112a) and using the fact that  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) are Hermitian operators, we find  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ = ⟨ Ψ | ( A ^ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ) ( B ^ − ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ = ⟨ Ψ ∣ ( A ^ B ^ − A ^ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ − B ^ ⟨ A ^ ⟩ + ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) Ψ ⟩ = ⟨ Ψ ∣ A ^ B ^ Ψ ⟩ − ⟨ Ψ ∣ A ^ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ Ψ ⟩ − ⟨ Ψ ∣ B ^ ⟨ A ^ ⟩ Ψ ⟩ + ⟨ Ψ ∣ ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ Ψ ⟩ = ⟨ A ^ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ + ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ = ⟨ A ^ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ . {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\langle f\mid g\rangle &=\langle \Psi |({\hat {A}}-\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle )({\hat {B}}-\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle \\\\[4pt]&=\langle \Psi \mid ({\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}-{\hat {A}}\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle -{\hat {B}}\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle +\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle )\Psi \rangle \\\\[4pt]&=\langle \Psi \mid {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\Psi \rangle -\langle \Psi \mid {\hat {A}}\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle \Psi \rangle -\langle \Psi \mid {\hat {B}}\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \Psi \rangle +\langle \Psi \mid \langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle \Psi \rangle \\\\[4pt]&=\langle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle +\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle \\\\[4pt]&=\langle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle .\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle &=\\langle \\Psi |\({\\hat {A}}-\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \)\({\\hat {B}}-\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle \\\\\[4pt\]&=\\langle \\Psi \\mid \({\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}-{\\hat {A}}\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle -{\\hat {B}}\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle +\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \)\\Psi \\rangle \\\\\[4pt\]&=\\langle \\Psi \\mid {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\Psi \\rangle -\\langle \\Psi \\mid {\\hat {A}}\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \\Psi \\rangle -\\langle \\Psi \\mid {\\hat {B}}\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\Psi \\rangle +\\langle \\Psi \\mid \\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \\Psi \\rangle \\\\\[4pt\]&=\\langle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle +\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle \\\\\[4pt\]&=\\langle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle .\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e8c0c05428ab53be785878d1546c265c45c6e755)
Similarly it can be shown that  ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ = ⟨ B ^ A ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ . {\displaystyle \langle g\mid f\rangle =\langle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle g\\mid f\\rangle =\\langle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/44768bec8f5b3e7dd57ab8f709ae2f5b7d98d0c4)
Thus, we have  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ − ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ = ⟨ A ^ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ B ^ A ^ ⟩ + ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ = ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle f\mid g\rangle -\langle g\mid f\rangle =\langle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\rangle +\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle =\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid g\\rangle -\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle =\\langle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\rangle +\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle =\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eaf12a5caf8e73c4e1cfdfbbf3ebb1c3e32e7e0f) and  ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ + ⟨ g ∣ f ⟩ = ⟨ A ^ B ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ + ⟨ B ^ A ^ ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ = ⟨ { A ^ , B ^ } ⟩ − 2 ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ . {\displaystyle \langle f\mid g\rangle +\langle g\mid f\rangle =\langle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle +\langle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle =\langle \\{{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}\\}\rangle -2\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f\\mid g\\rangle +\\langle g\\mid f\\rangle =\\langle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle +\\langle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle =\\langle \\{{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\\}\\rangle -2\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b072e1951cc16b764a06a15ea531f69c4c74dffb)
We now substitute the above two equations above back into Eq. (**[4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#math_4)**) and get  | ⟨ f ∣ g ⟩ | 2 = ( 1 2 ⟨ { A ^ , B ^ } ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) 2 + ( 1 2 i ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ ) 2 . {\displaystyle |\langle f\mid g\rangle |^{2}={\Big (}{\frac {1}{2}}\langle \\{{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}\\}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle {\Big )}^{2}+{\Big (}{\frac {1}{2i}}\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle {\Big )}^{2}\,.} ![{\\displaystyle |\\langle f\\mid g\\rangle |^{2}={\\Big \(}{\\frac {1}{2}}\\langle \\{{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\\}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle {\\Big \)}^{2}+{\\Big \(}{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle {\\Big \)}^{2}\\,.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/074cd206c3b6789fd747fa2efa892ea596651470)
Substituting the above into Equation (**[2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#math_2)**) we get the Schrödinger uncertainty relation  σ A σ B ≥ ( 1 2 ⟨ { A ^ , B ^ } ⟩ − ⟨ A ^ ⟩ ⟨ B ^ ⟩ ) 2 + ( 1 2 i ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ ) 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}\sigma _{B}\geq {\sqrt {{\Big (}{\frac {1}{2}}\langle \\{{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}\\}\rangle -\langle {\hat {A}}\rangle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle {\Big )}^{2}+{\Big (}{\frac {1}{2i}}\langle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle {\Big )}^{2}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}\\sigma _{B}\\geq {\\sqrt {{\\Big \(}{\\frac {1}{2}}\\langle \\{{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\\}\\rangle -\\langle {\\hat {A}}\\rangle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle {\\Big \)}^{2}+{\\Big \(}{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\langle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle {\\Big \)}^{2}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/96a2a724edaa3e9b3e175f7de8158b796bef58c0)
This proof has an issue[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-19) related to the domains of the operators involved. For the proof to make sense, the vector  B ^ | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}|\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}|\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f356e37b906e9f3a48fe46b0d5da9493dcebee00) has to be in the domain of the [unbounded operator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unbounded_operator "Unbounded operator") A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821), which is not always the case. In fact, the Robertson uncertainty relation is false if  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) is an angle variable and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) is the derivative with respect to this variable.
