In this example, the commutator is a nonzero constant—just as in the Heisenberg uncertainty relation—and yet there are states where the product of the uncertainties is zero.[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Hall2013-20) (See the counterexample section below.) This issue can be overcome by using a [variational method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variational_method "Variational method") for the proof,[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Jackiw-21)[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-CarruthersNieto-22) or by working with an exponentiated version of the canonical commutation relations.[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Hall2013-20)
Note that in the general form of the Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty relation, there is no need to assume that the operators  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) are [self-adjoint operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-adjoint_operator#Self-adjoint_operators "Self-adjoint operator"). It suffices to assume that they are merely [symmetric operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-adjoint_operator#Symmetric_operators "Self-adjoint operator"). (The distinction between these two notions is generally glossed over in the physics literature, where the term _Hermitian_ is used for either or both classes of operators. See Chapter 9 of Hall's book[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-23) for a detailed discussion of this important but technical distinction.) 
### Phase space
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Phase space")]
In the [phase space formulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_space_formulation "Phase space formulation") of quantum mechanics, the Robertson–Schrödinger relation follows from a positivity condition on a real star-square function. Given a [Wigner function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigner_quasi-probability_distribution "Wigner quasi-probability distribution") W ( x , p ) {\displaystyle W(x,p)} ![{\\displaystyle W\(x,p\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eb40b06d50873edbe7c0f962a6ed614650d550b1) with [star product](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moyal_product "Moyal product") ★ and a function _f_ , the following is generally true:[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-24) ⟨ f ∗ ⋆ f ⟩ = ∫ ( f ∗ ⋆ f ) W ( x , p ) d x d p ≥ 0 . {\displaystyle \langle f^{*}\star f\rangle =\int (f^{*}\star f)\,W(x,p)\,dx\,dp\geq 0~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f^{*}\\star f\\rangle =\\int \(f^{*}\\star f\)\\,W\(x,p\)\\,dx\\,dp\\geq 0~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6176d1d939f0013efb6f9850e6a7cb7f13bb586b)
Choosing  f = a + b x + c p {\displaystyle f=a+bx+cp} ![{\\displaystyle f=a+bx+cp}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/698e38f2e445599645f5cd4106c734daa8b4eca6), we arrive at  ⟨ f ∗ ⋆ f ⟩ = [ a ∗ b ∗ c ∗ ] [ 1 ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ x ⋆ x ⟩ ⟨ x ⋆ p ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ p ⋆ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⋆ p ⟩ ] [ a b c ] ≥ 0 . {\displaystyle \langle f^{*}\star f\rangle ={\begin{bmatrix}a^{*}&b^{*}&c^{*}\end{bmatrix}}{\begin{bmatrix}1&\langle x\rangle &\langle p\rangle \\\\\langle x\rangle &\langle x\star x\rangle &\langle x\star p\rangle \\\\\langle p\rangle &\langle p\star x\rangle &\langle p\star p\rangle \end{bmatrix}}{\begin{bmatrix}a\\\b\\\c\end{bmatrix}}\geq 0~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle f^{*}\\star f\\rangle ={\\begin{bmatrix}a^{*}&b^{*}&c^{*}\\end{bmatrix}}{\\begin{bmatrix}1&\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle p\\rangle \\\\\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle x\\star x\\rangle &\\langle x\\star p\\rangle \\\\\\langle p\\rangle &\\langle p\\star x\\rangle &\\langle p\\star p\\rangle \\end{bmatrix}}{\\begin{bmatrix}a\\\\b\\\\c\\end{bmatrix}}\\geq 0~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b84cbd0406a059f6665b9dc8769f3615d30aa3a5)
Since this positivity condition is true for _all_ _a_ , _b_ , and _c_ , it follows that all the eigenvalues of the matrix are non-negative. 
The non-negative eigenvalues then imply a corresponding non-negativity condition on the [determinant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Determinant "Determinant"),  det [ 1 ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ x ⋆ x ⟩ ⟨ x ⋆ p ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ p ⋆ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⋆ p ⟩ ] = det [ 1 ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ x 2 ⟩ ⟨ x p + i ℏ 2 ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ⟨ x p − i ℏ 2 ⟩ ⟨ p 2 ⟩ ] ≥ 0 , {\displaystyle \det {\begin{bmatrix}1&\langle x\rangle &\langle p\rangle \\\\\langle x\rangle &\langle x\star x\rangle &\langle x\star p\rangle \\\\\langle p\rangle &\langle p\star x\rangle &\langle p\star p\rangle \end{bmatrix}}=\det {\begin{bmatrix}1&\langle x\rangle &\langle p\rangle \\\\\langle x\rangle &\langle x^{2}\rangle &\left\langle xp+{\frac {i\hbar }{2}}\right\rangle \\\\\langle p\rangle &\left\langle xp-{\frac {i\hbar }{2}}\right\rangle &\langle p^{2}\rangle \end{bmatrix}}\geq 0~,} ![{\\displaystyle \\det {\\begin{bmatrix}1&\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle p\\rangle \\\\\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle x\\star x\\rangle &\\langle x\\star p\\rangle \\\\\\langle p\\rangle &\\langle p\\star x\\rangle &\\langle p\\star p\\rangle \\end{bmatrix}}=\\det {\\begin{bmatrix}1&\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle p\\rangle \\\\\\langle x\\rangle &\\langle x^{2}\\rangle &\\left\\langle xp+{\\frac {i\\hbar }{2}}\\right\\rangle \\\\\\langle p\\rangle &\\left\\langle xp-{\\frac {i\\hbar }{2}}\\right\\rangle &\\langle p^{2}\\rangle \\end{bmatrix}}\\geq 0~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6c844f47cab0244a8d1ed409a5a7b7b6bc339435) or, explicitly, after algebraic manipulation,  σ x 2 σ p 2 = ( ⟨ x 2 ⟩ − ⟨ x ⟩ 2 ) ( ⟨ p 2 ⟩ − ⟨ p ⟩ 2 ) ≥ ( ⟨ x p ⟩ − ⟨ x ⟩ ⟨ p ⟩ ) 2 + ℏ 2 4 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}^{2}\sigma _{p}^{2}=\left(\langle x^{2}\rangle -\langle x\rangle ^{2}\right)\left(\langle p^{2}\rangle -\langle p\rangle ^{2}\right)\geq \left(\langle xp\rangle -\langle x\rangle \langle p\rangle \right)^{2}+{\frac {\hbar ^{2}}{4}}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}^{2}\\sigma _{p}^{2}=\\left\(\\langle x^{2}\\rangle -\\langle x\\rangle ^{2}\\right\)\\left\(\\langle p^{2}\\rangle -\\langle p\\rangle ^{2}\\right\)\\geq \\left\(\\langle xp\\rangle -\\langle x\\rangle \\langle p\\rangle \\right\)^{2}+{\\frac {\\hbar ^{2}}{4}}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1f7cb1468a0109c36f89ab5abeebfb879aee78a8)
### Examples
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Examples")]
Since the Robertson and Schrödinger relations are for general operators, the relations can be applied to any two observables to obtain specific uncertainty relations. A few of the most common relations found in the literature are given below. 
  * **Position–linear momentum uncertainty relation** : for the position and linear momentum operators, the canonical commutation relation  [ x ^ , p ^ ] = i ℏ {\displaystyle [{\hat {x}},{\hat {p}}]=i\hbar } ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {x}},{\\hat {p}}\]=i\\hbar }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/42dbbd0db710385288536bcf4f4a1b7cceb75d9a) implies the Kennard inequality from above:  σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/836dad4643586ae81075d9a01cc21d12b6f503da)
  * **Angular momentum uncertainty relation** : For two orthogonal components of the [total angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum "Angular momentum") operator of an object:  σ J i σ J j ≥ ℏ 2 | ⟨ J k ⟩ | , {\displaystyle \sigma _{J_{i}}\sigma _{J_{j}}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}{\big |}\langle J_{k}\rangle {\big |},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{J_{i}}\\sigma _{J_{j}}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}{\\big |}\\langle J_{k}\\rangle {\\big |},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4169bed178cd0aad0a07a5ee7af596f5cc9aedc5) where _i_ , _j_ , _k_ are distinct, and _J_ _i_ denotes angular momentum along the _x_ _i_ axis. This relation implies that unless all three components vanish together, only a single component of a system's angular momentum can be defined with arbitrary precision, normally the component parallel to an external (magnetic or electric) field. Moreover, for  [ J x , J y ] = i ℏ ε x y z J z {\displaystyle [J_{x},J_{y}]=i\hbar \varepsilon _{xyz}J_{z}} ![{\\displaystyle \[J_{x},J_{y}\]=i\\hbar \\varepsilon _{xyz}J_{z}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/92feb40a1e1bb732beffa55c687ccfbcec8ff007), a choice  A ^ = J x {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}=J_{x}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}=J_{x}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/39709b534708a5754d72a8bc198c646f72cb70a5),  B ^ = J y {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}=J_{y}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}=J_{y}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/82447267c48d20721d864f831ef924d107a04eba), in angular momentum multiplets, _ψ_ = |_j_ , _m_ ⟩, bounds the [Casimir invariant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Casimir_invariant "Casimir invariant") (angular momentum squared,  ⟨ J x 2 + J y 2 + J z 2 ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/55625eafb8662fdb7a572a3a25c9ab5efd313a3f)) from below and thus yields useful constraints such as _j_(_j_ + 1) ≥ _m_(_m_ + 1), and hence _j_ ≥ _m_ , among others.


  * For the number of electrons in a [superconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superconductor "Superconductor") and the [phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_factor "Phase factor") of its [Ginzburg–Landau order parameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginzburg%E2%80%93Landau_theory "Ginzburg–Landau theory")[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-25)[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-26) Δ N Δ φ ≥ 1. {\displaystyle \Delta N\,\Delta \varphi \geq 1.} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta N\\,\\Delta \\varphi \\geq 1.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/31734f1fdfdbcff412a201cd1e77ce47a35c0396)


### Limitations
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Limitations")]
The derivation of the Robertson inequality for operators  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) requires  A ^ B ^ ψ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\psi } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7756f1784424b7a0368e5614355400232ebb7961) and  B ^ A ^ ψ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\psi } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/35ef9d2b4cc00831b4cf6ca917cb96b99bc6f25a) to be defined. There are quantum systems where these conditions are not valid.[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-27) One example is a quantum [particle on a ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_in_a_ring "Particle in a ring"), where the wave function depends on an angular variable  θ {\displaystyle \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5ab2664b422d53eb0c7df3b87e1360d75ad9af) in the interval  [ 0 , 2 π ] {\displaystyle [0,2\pi ]} ![{\\displaystyle \[0,2\\pi \]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/348d40bf3f8b7e1c00c4346440d7e2e4f0cc9b91). Define "position" and "momentum" operators  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) by  A ^ ψ ( θ ) = θ ψ ( θ ) , θ ∈ [ 0 , 2 π ] , {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}\psi (\theta )=\theta \psi (\theta ),\quad \theta \in [0,2\pi ],} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}\\psi \(\\theta \)=\\theta \\psi \(\\theta \),\\quad \\theta \\in \[0,2\\pi \],}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f85b2a63d69030bd4deabafe7641563413b8ec23) and  B ^ ψ = − i ℏ d ψ d θ , {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}\psi =-i\hbar {\frac {d\psi }{d\theta }},} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}\\psi =-i\\hbar {\\frac {d\\psi }{d\\theta }},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ce80f48ffb1b35350af9e80e7ce923764351a3a1) with periodic boundary conditions on  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38). The definition of  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) depends the  θ {\displaystyle \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5ab2664b422d53eb0c7df3b87e1360d75ad9af) range from 0 to  2 π {\displaystyle 2\pi } ![{\\displaystyle 2\\pi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/73efd1f6493490b058097060a572606d2c550a06). These operators satisfy the usual commutation relations for position and momentum operators,  [ A ^ , B ^ ] = i ℏ {\displaystyle [{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}]=i\hbar } ![{\\displaystyle \[{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}\]=i\\hbar }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ac8c6748f07eed66c5b329fcebdb8ca9d859ff71). More precisely,  A ^ B ^ ψ − B ^ A ^ ψ = i ℏ ψ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\psi -{\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\psi =i\hbar \psi } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\psi -{\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\psi =i\\hbar \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/03b5084d68bc9a302e622ed1000668c7a88ccce3) whenever both  A ^ B ^ ψ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}{\hat {B}}\psi } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}{\\hat {B}}\\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7756f1784424b7a0368e5614355400232ebb7961) and  B ^ A ^ ψ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}\psi } ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}\\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/35ef9d2b4cc00831b4cf6ca917cb96b99bc6f25a) are defined, and the space of such  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a) is a dense subspace of the quantum Hilbert space.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-28)
Now let  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a) be any of the eigenstates of  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38), which are given by  ψ ( θ ) = e 2 π i n θ {\displaystyle \psi (\theta )=e^{2\pi in\theta }} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(\\theta \)=e^{2\\pi in\\theta }}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1547b424251069f64de03735f7aad18d71dc4183). These states are normalizable, unlike the eigenstates of the momentum operator on the line. Also the operator  A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f595a6c73d1183d6a1b2ac21fe47ac28c1483821) is bounded, since  θ {\displaystyle \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5ab2664b422d53eb0c7df3b87e1360d75ad9af) ranges over a bounded interval. Thus, in the state  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a), the uncertainty of  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a) is zero and the uncertainty of  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3) is finite, so that  σ A σ B = 0. {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}\sigma _{B}=0.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}\\sigma _{B}=0.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e91929bba5ac63ebe76e8685d94a529ecf1193c3) The Robertson uncertainty principle does not apply in this case:  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a) is not in the domain of the operator  B ^ A ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}{\hat {A}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}{\\hat {A}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cb714209e8169c0ef0b37866f83f604be9d7954a), since multiplication by  θ {\displaystyle \theta } ![{\\displaystyle \\theta }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e5ab2664b422d53eb0c7df3b87e1360d75ad9af) disrupts the periodic boundary conditions imposed on  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38).[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Hall2013-20)
For the usual position and momentum operators  X ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {X}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {X}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/acc59ad6d9a06d55b96b65beb0fdfc89acc1e40e) and  P ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {P}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {P}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7a46a8cf7bc789e8c4f8de7ca0d9946a46fb8842) on the real line, no such counterexamples can occur. As long as  σ x {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ebf4ab08fe163a6c495cad6f4d67653287c4044c) and  σ p {\displaystyle \sigma _{p}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{p}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cd141c4b4b162275b62a7b903a1a065271fae3cb) are defined in the state  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a), the Heisenberg uncertainty principle holds, even if  ψ {\displaystyle \psi } ![{\\displaystyle \\psi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/45e5789e5d9c8f7c79744f43ecaaf8ba42a8553a) fails to be in the domain of  X ^ P ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {X}}{\hat {P}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {X}}{\\hat {P}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0fb6b8e37140f3760ff055fee1f6841ced6a86d3) or of  P ^ X ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {P}}{\hat {X}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {P}}{\\hat {X}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9287386ae96b9fb320526a87d8031eb007577cb4).[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-29)
### Mixed states
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: Mixed states")]
The Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty can be improved noting that it must hold for all components  ϱ k {\displaystyle \varrho _{k}} ![{\\displaystyle \\varrho _{k}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fd820c749636270da83f0201b3c7f0f0c5813da9) in any decomposition of the [density matrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Density_matrix "Density matrix") given as  ϱ = ∑ k p k ϱ k . {\displaystyle \varrho =\sum _{k}p_{k}\varrho _{k}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\varrho =\\sum _{k}p_{k}\\varrho _{k}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5008b83c3205b1c14afaf4afec667abf1d5a6a4b) Here, for the probabilities  p k ≥ 0 {\displaystyle p_{k}\geq 0} ![{\\displaystyle p_{k}\\geq 0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c211534e2bd36d1d268391597803cfc89b76446e) and  ∑ k p k = 1 {\displaystyle \sum _{k}p_{k}=1} ![{\\displaystyle \\sum _{k}p_{k}=1}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bcf8e8a35fbfb43f8a899464cd55283daad063e8) hold. Then, using the relation  ∑ k a k ∑ k b k ≥ ( ∑ k a k b k ) 2 {\displaystyle \sum _{k}a_{k}\sum _{k}b_{k}\geq \left(\sum _{k}{\sqrt {a_{k}b_{k}}}\right)^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sum _{k}a_{k}\\sum _{k}b_{k}\\geq \\left\(\\sum _{k}{\\sqrt {a_{k}b_{k}}}\\right\)^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9f2d2843a36a11e3c83c8853df73ae50274035bb) for  a k , b k ≥ 0 {\displaystyle a_{k},b_{k}\geq 0} ![{\\displaystyle a_{k},b_{k}\\geq 0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d1b2908b8f3e0ffde870351b93b769a941cafe8a), it follows that[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-PhysRevResearch21-30) σ A 2 σ B 2 ≥ [ ∑ k p k L ( ϱ k ) ] 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq \left[\sum _{k}p_{k}L(\varrho _{k})\right]^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq \\left\[\\sum _{k}p_{k}L\(\\varrho _{k}\)\\right\]^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fe2197885d47337c2e848acc051efdea71b02870) where the function in the bound is defined  L ( ϱ ) = | 1 2 tr ⁡ ( ρ { A , B } ) − tr ⁡ ( ρ A ) tr ⁡ ( ρ B ) | 2 + | 1 2 i tr ⁡ ( ρ [ A , B ] ) | 2 . {\displaystyle L(\varrho )={\sqrt {\left|{\frac {1}{2}}\operatorname {tr} (\rho \\{A,B\\})-\operatorname {tr} (\rho A)\operatorname {tr} (\rho B)\right|^{2}+\left|{\frac {1}{2i}}\operatorname {tr} (\rho [A,B])\right|^{2}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle L\(\\varrho \)={\\sqrt {\\left|{\\frac {1}{2}}\\operatorname {tr} \(\\rho \\{A,B\\}\)-\\operatorname {tr} \(\\rho A\)\\operatorname {tr} \(\\rho B\)\\right|^{2}+\\left|{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\operatorname {tr} \(\\rho \[A,B\]\)\\right|^{2}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9217563cc15d7b37797888126f4b4c44b7f19c08) The above relation very often has a bound larger than that of the original Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty relation. Thus, we need to calculate the bound of the Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty for the mixed components of the quantum state rather than for the quantum state, and compute an average of their square roots. The following expression is stronger than the Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty relation  σ A 2 σ B 2 ≥ [ max p k , ϱ k ∑ k p k L ( ϱ k ) ] 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq \left[\max _{p_{k},\varrho _{k}}\sum _{k}p_{k}L(\varrho _{k})\right]^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq \\left\[\\max _{p_{k},\\varrho _{k}}\\sum _{k}p_{k}L\(\\varrho _{k}\)\\right\]^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/98f249b302c95aa4be14de7ec6f82ffb5f169d5e) where on the right-hand side there is a concave roof over the decompositions of the density matrix. The improved relation above is saturated by all single-qubit quantum states.[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-PhysRevResearch21-30)
With similar arguments, one can derive a relation with a convex roof on the right-hand side[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-PhysRevResearch21-30) σ A 2 F Q [ ϱ , B ] ≥ 4 [ min p k , Ψ k ∑ k p k L ( | Ψ k ⟩ ⟨ Ψ k | ) ] 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}F_{Q}[\varrho ,B]\geq 4\left[\min _{p_{k},\Psi _{k}}\sum _{k}p_{k}L(\vert \Psi _{k}\rangle \langle \Psi _{k}\vert )\right]^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,B\]\\geq 4\\left\[\\min _{p_{k},\\Psi _{k}}\\sum _{k}p_{k}L\(\\vert \\Psi _{k}\\rangle \\langle \\Psi _{k}\\vert \)\\right\]^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/58902ccc3a0b70047c4db2a60db2c59e012fd34f) where  F Q [ ϱ , B ] {\displaystyle F_{Q}[\varrho ,B]} ![{\\displaystyle F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,B\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7022310dbb24400e5c70f2a78e6f35633b161a2a) denotes the [quantum Fisher information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_Fisher_information "Quantum Fisher information") and the density matrix is decomposed to pure states as  ϱ = ∑ k p k | Ψ k ⟩ ⟨ Ψ k | . {\displaystyle \varrho =\sum _{k}p_{k}\vert \Psi _{k}\rangle \langle \Psi _{k}\vert .} ![{\\displaystyle \\varrho =\\sum _{k}p_{k}\\vert \\Psi _{k}\\rangle \\langle \\Psi _{k}\\vert .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e3a2527e91036785fb097d66dfe0a0d8d88bea3) The derivation takes advantage of the fact that the [quantum Fisher information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_Fisher_information "Quantum Fisher information") is the convex roof of the variance times four.[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-31)[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-32)
A simpler inequality follows without a convex roof[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-33) σ A 2 F Q [ ϱ , B ] ≥ | ⟨ i [ A , B ] ⟩ | 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}F_{Q}[\varrho ,B]\geq \vert \langle i[A,B]\rangle \vert ^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,B\]\\geq \\vert \\langle i\[A,B\]\\rangle \\vert ^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c25345e515fab68aaa473847b0f644f1d1b0965a) which is stronger than the Heisenberg uncertainty relation, since for the quantum Fisher information we have  F Q [ ϱ , B ] ≤ 4 σ B , {\displaystyle F_{Q}[\varrho ,B]\leq 4\sigma _{B},} ![{\\displaystyle F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,B\]\\leq 4\\sigma _{B},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/76b697790877dcd32b480f16ef3565113c062e83) while for pure states the equality holds. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=16 "Edit section: The Maccone–Pati uncertainty relations")]
The Robertson–Schrödinger uncertainty relation can be trivial if the state of the system is chosen to be eigenstate of one of the observable. The stronger uncertainty relations proved by Lorenzo Maccone and [Arun K. Pati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arun_K._Pati "Arun K. Pati") give non-trivial bounds on the sum of the variances for two incompatible observables.[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-34) (Earlier works on uncertainty relations formulated as the sum of variances include, e.g., Ref.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-35) due to Yichen Huang.) For two non-commuting observables  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3) and  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a) the first stronger uncertainty relation is given by  σ A 2 + σ B 2 ≥ ± i ⟨ Ψ ∣ [ A , B ] | Ψ ⟩ + ∣ ⟨ Ψ ∣ ( A ± i B ) ∣ Ψ ¯ ⟩ | 2 , {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}+\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq \pm i\langle \Psi \mid [A,B]|\Psi \rangle +\mid \langle \Psi \mid (A\pm iB)\mid {\bar {\Psi }}\rangle |^{2},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}+\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq \\pm i\\langle \\Psi \\mid \[A,B\]|\\Psi \\rangle +\\mid \\langle \\Psi \\mid \(A\\pm iB\)\\mid {\\bar {\\Psi }}\\rangle |^{2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fe87035b4463c5578ccda71d74b8e7c9fd627515) where  σ A 2 = ⟨ Ψ | A 2 | Ψ ⟩ − ⟨ Ψ ∣ A ∣ Ψ ⟩ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}=\langle \Psi |A^{2}|\Psi \rangle -\langle \Psi \mid A\mid \Psi \rangle ^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}=\\langle \\Psi |A^{2}|\\Psi \\rangle -\\langle \\Psi \\mid A\\mid \\Psi \\rangle ^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5ae685b668693d6bd8cb15b97e4434243ae0aa1e),  σ B 2 = ⟨ Ψ | B 2 | Ψ ⟩ − ⟨ Ψ ∣ B ∣ Ψ ⟩ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{B}^{2}=\langle \Psi |B^{2}|\Psi \rangle -\langle \Psi \mid B\mid \Psi \rangle ^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{B}^{2}=\\langle \\Psi |B^{2}|\\Psi \\rangle -\\langle \\Psi \\mid B\\mid \\Psi \\rangle ^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cdebc4bf7859b83a8faec0ce625fc2a65d162b85),  | Ψ ¯ ⟩ {\displaystyle |{\bar {\Psi }}\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |{\\bar {\\Psi }}\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e1b3636e04176d1e27cadef7f693609ed5de4387) is a normalized vector that is orthogonal to the state of the system  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c) and one should choose the sign of  ± i ⟨ Ψ ∣ [ A , B ] ∣ Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle \pm i\langle \Psi \mid [A,B]\mid \Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\pm i\\langle \\Psi \\mid \[A,B\]\\mid \\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3a34a5250780e2a23b4befafff39d5522cc8266c) to make this real quantity a positive number. 
The second stronger uncertainty relation is given by  σ A 2 + σ B 2 ≥ 1 2 | ⟨ Ψ ¯ A + B ∣ ( A + B ) ∣ Ψ ⟩ | 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}^{2}+\sigma _{B}^{2}\geq {\frac {1}{2}}|\langle {\bar {\Psi }}_{A+B}\mid (A+B)\mid \Psi \rangle |^{2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}^{2}+\\sigma _{B}^{2}\\geq {\\frac {1}{2}}|\\langle {\\bar {\\Psi }}_{A+B}\\mid \(A+B\)\\mid \\Psi \\rangle |^{2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/46532c7d12491c94362c77722658c06fc4a7c40c) where  | Ψ ¯ A + B ⟩ {\displaystyle |{\bar {\Psi }}_{A+B}\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |{\\bar {\\Psi }}_{A+B}\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ff187f95e120bf19939fcd4574b86842f97357d3) is a state orthogonal to  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c). The form of  | Ψ ¯ A + B ⟩ {\displaystyle |{\bar {\Psi }}_{A+B}\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |{\\bar {\\Psi }}_{A+B}\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ff187f95e120bf19939fcd4574b86842f97357d3) implies that the right-hand side of the new uncertainty relation is nonzero unless  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c) is an eigenstate of  ( A + B ) {\displaystyle (A+B)} ![{\\displaystyle \(A+B\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9ecb0157ba273d1d4eefa5af72b1c73748db7234). One may note that  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c) can be an eigenstate of  ( A + B ) {\displaystyle (A+B)} ![{\\displaystyle \(A+B\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9ecb0157ba273d1d4eefa5af72b1c73748db7234) without being an eigenstate of either  A {\displaystyle A} ![{\\displaystyle A}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7daff47fa58cdfd29dc333def748ff5fa4c923e3) or  B {\displaystyle B} ![{\\displaystyle B}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/47136aad860d145f75f3eed3022df827cee94d7a). However, when  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c) is an eigenstate of one of the two observables the Heisenberg–Schrödinger uncertainty relation becomes trivial. But the lower bound in the new relation is nonzero unless  | Ψ ⟩ {\displaystyle |\Psi \rangle } ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6e77f6b1e903837c5765c9683da41dd93199621c) is an eigenstate of both. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=17 "Edit section: Energy–time")]
Δ E Δ t ≳ ℏ / 2 , {\displaystyle \Delta E\Delta t\gtrsim \hbar /2,} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E\\Delta t\\gtrsim \\hbar /2,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/53450b6764bdbfbcf4cd50da9c8ca72f15442274) has a long, controversial history; the meaning of  Δ t {\displaystyle \Delta t} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta t}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8c28867ecd34e2caed12cf38feadf6a81a7ee542) and  Δ E {\displaystyle \Delta E} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/345f0ed5ff6ec6eddc5f908d379f032c52f119c2) varies and different formulations have different arenas of validity.[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36) However, one well-known application is both well established[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-37)[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Hilgevoord-38) and experimentally verified:[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-39)[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-40) the connection between the life-time of a resonance state,  τ 1 / 2 {\displaystyle \tau _{\sqrt {1/2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau _{\\sqrt {1/2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d0d04b6898432f7ea34c7c3d765e217a7a24671b) and its energy width  Δ E {\displaystyle \Delta E} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/345f0ed5ff6ec6eddc5f908d379f032c52f119c2):  τ 1 / 2 Δ E = π ℏ / 4. {\displaystyle \tau _{\sqrt {1/2}}\Delta E=\pi \hbar /4.} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau _{\\sqrt {1/2}}\\Delta E=\\pi \\hbar /4.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b922ef4f3fd8cadb305b36191a4e1d164f35944d) In particle-physics, widths from experimental fits to the [Breit–Wigner energy distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relativistic_Breit%E2%80%93Wigner_distribution "Relativistic Breit–Wigner distribution") are used to characterize the lifetime of quasi-stable or decaying states.[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-41)
An informal, heuristic meaning of the principle is the following:[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-42) A state that only exists for a short time cannot have a definite energy. To have a definite energy, the frequency of the state must be defined accurately, and this requires the state to hang around for many cycles, the reciprocal of the required accuracy. For example, in [spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_spectroscopy "Electromagnetic spectroscopy"), excited states have a finite lifetime. By the time–energy uncertainty principle, they do not have a definite energy, and, each time they decay, the energy they release is slightly different. The average energy of the outgoing photon has a peak at the theoretical energy of the state, but the distribution has a finite width called the [_natural linewidth_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectral_linewidth "Spectral linewidth"). Fast-decaying states have a broad linewidth, while slow-decaying states have a narrow linewidth.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-43) The same linewidth effect also makes it difficult to specify the [rest mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rest_mass "Rest mass") of unstable, fast-decaying particles in [particle physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_physics "Particle physics"). The faster the [particle decays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_decay "Particle decay") (the shorter its lifetime), the less certain is its mass (the larger the particle's [width](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resonance_\(particle_physics\) "Resonance \(particle physics\)")). 
### Time in quantum mechanics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=18 "Edit section: Time in quantum mechanics")]
The concept of "time" in quantum mechanics offers many challenges.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-HilgevoordConfusion-44) There is no quantum theory of time measurement; relativity is both fundamental to time and difficult to include in quantum mechanics.[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36) While position and momentum are associated with a single particle, time is a system property: it has no operator needed for the Robertson–Schrödinger relation.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1) The mathematical treatment of stable and unstable quantum systems differ.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-45) These factors combine to make energy–time uncertainty principles controversial. 
Three notions of "time" can be distinguished:[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36) external, intrinsic, and observable. External or laboratory time is seen by the experimenter; intrinsic time is inferred by changes in dynamic variables, like the hands of a clock or the motion of a [free particle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_particle "Free particle"); observable time concerns time as an observable, the measurement of time-separated events. 
An external-time energy–time uncertainty principle might say that measuring the energy of a quantum system to an accuracy  Δ E {\displaystyle \Delta E} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/345f0ed5ff6ec6eddc5f908d379f032c52f119c2) requires a time interval  Δ t > h / Δ E {\displaystyle \Delta t>h/\Delta E} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta t>h/\\Delta E}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/db829d14bb062df0bfb46ab33f8ad2d5dfcd1e4b).[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Hilgevoord-38) However, [Yakir Aharonov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yakir_Aharonov "Yakir Aharonov") and [David Bohm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Bohm "David Bohm")[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-46)[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36) have shown that, in some quantum systems, energy can be measured accurately within an arbitrarily short time: external-time uncertainty principles are not universal. 
Intrinsic time is the basis for several formulations of energy–time uncertainty relations, including the Mandelstam–Tamm relation discussed in the next section. A physical system with an intrinsic time closely matching the external laboratory time is called a "clock".[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-HilgevoordConfusion-44): 31 
Observable time, measuring time between two events, remains a challenge for quantum theories; some progress has been made using [ positive operator-valued measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/POVM "POVM") concepts.[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=19 "Edit section: Mandelstam–Tamm")]
In 1945, [Leonid Mandelstam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonid_Mandelstam "Leonid Mandelstam") and [Igor Tamm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Igor_Tamm "Igor Tamm") derived a non-relativistic _time–energy uncertainty relation_ as follows.[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-47)[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Busch2002-36) From Heisenberg mechanics, the generalized [Ehrenfest theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ehrenfest_theorem "Ehrenfest theorem") for an observable _B_ without explicit time dependence, represented by a self-adjoint operator  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) relates time dependence of the average value of  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38) to the average of its commutator with the Hamiltonian: 
d ⟨ B ^ ⟩ d t = i ℏ ⟨ [ H ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ . {\displaystyle {\frac {d\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle }{dt}}={\frac {i}{\hbar }}\langle [{\hat {H}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle .} ![{\\displaystyle {\\frac {d\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle }{dt}}={\\frac {i}{\\hbar }}\\langle \[{\\hat {H}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle .}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/92bca95fec448b2e3291ae56caf76d1796e6c02f)
The value of  ⟨ [ H ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ {\displaystyle \langle [{\hat {H}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle \\langle \[{\\hat {H}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/42ce91773492f7d6bdcd2ce0ad93cb92f38c1287) is then substituted in the [Robertson uncertainty relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#Robertson%E2%80%93Schr%C3%B6dinger_uncertainty_relations) for the energy operator  H ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {H}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {H}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6bb06de5217295d7fbdbf68fb9c5309a513fc99e) and  B ^ {\displaystyle {\hat {B}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\hat {B}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ffa3bc40e590b7dfa0cd144e13163abf26e5c38):  σ H σ B ≥ | 1 2 i ⟨ [ H ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | , {\displaystyle \sigma _{H}\sigma _{B}\geq \left|{\frac {1}{2i}}\langle [{\hat {H}},{\hat {B}}]\rangle \right|,} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{H}\\sigma _{B}\\geq \\left|{\\frac {1}{2i}}\\langle \[{\\hat {H}},{\\hat {B}}\]\\rangle \\right|,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/48c8d45afe6c1f61a750dbb97358c10dde03f8fb) giving  σ H σ B | d ⟨ B ^ ⟩ d t | ≥ ℏ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{H}{\frac {\sigma _{B}}{\left|{\frac {d\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle }{dt}}\right|}}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{H}{\\frac {\\sigma _{B}}{\\left|{\\frac {d\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle }{dt}}\\right|}}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b523c06f10cb57a983e8aad310c9f4f87c66bfae) (whenever the denominator is nonzero). While this is a universal result, it depends upon the observable chosen and that the deviations  σ H {\displaystyle \sigma _{H}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{H}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3b44dc14bfe05974485891417ad549057a6385d5) and  σ B {\displaystyle \sigma _{B}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6daf3fafcc7df4a3fda4f23f5c09b9c3944c346f) are computed for a particular state. Identifying  Δ E ≡ σ E {\displaystyle \Delta E\equiv \sigma _{E}} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E\\equiv \\sigma _{E}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eff56367f4d864b5a81fd5d52084b5302efc30bd) and the characteristic time  τ B ≡ σ B | d ⟨ B ^ ⟩ d t | {\displaystyle \tau _{B}\equiv {\frac {\sigma _{B}}{\left|{\frac {d\langle {\hat {B}}\rangle }{dt}}\right|}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau _{B}\\equiv {\\frac {\\sigma _{B}}{\\left|{\\frac {d\\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle }{dt}}\\right|}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/53c3e23f79bdbfb4ca7698666cf22627faf03ff6) gives an energy–time relationship  Δ E τ B ≥ ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \Delta E\tau _{B}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta E\\tau _{B}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/548efe2287fcd2e5b955fc12e7ac51c439a17981) Although  τ B {\displaystyle \tau _{B}} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau _{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8360a39672c98a2b406ae3d99eb30531703ed043) has the dimension of time, it is different from the time parameter _t_ that enters the [Schrödinger equation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schr%C3%B6dinger_equation "Schrödinger equation"). This  τ B {\displaystyle \tau _{B}} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau _{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8360a39672c98a2b406ae3d99eb30531703ed043) can be interpreted as time for which the expectation value of the observable,  ⟨ B ^ ⟩ , {\displaystyle \langle {\hat {B}}\rangle ,} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle {\\hat {B}}\\rangle ,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/af3d5e5522d08a723483b3692d74327a5ea1b566) changes by an amount equal to one standard deviation.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-48) Examples: 
  * The time a free quantum particle passes a point in space is more uncertain as the energy of the state is more precisely controlled:  Δ T = ℏ / 2 Δ E . {\displaystyle \Delta T=\hbar /2\Delta E.} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta T=\\hbar /2\\Delta E.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cc9ad2573cd0e8aade9c91fbdae58266640ad2a1) Since the time spread is related to the particle position spread and the energy spread is related to the momentum spread, this relation is directly related to position–momentum uncertainty.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GriffithsSchroeter2018-17): 144 
  * A [Delta particle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delta_particle "Delta particle"), a quasistable composite of quarks related to protons and neutrons, has a lifetime of 10−23 s, so its measured [ mass equivalent to energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass%E2%80%93energy_equivalence "Mass–energy equivalence"), 1232 MeV/_c_ 2, varies by ±120 MeV/_c_ 2; this variation is intrinsic and not caused by measurement errors.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GriffithsSchroeter2018-17): 144 
  * Two energy states  ψ 1 , 2 {\displaystyle \psi _{1,2}} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi _{1,2}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f374739cd584a31565a34b18ad9a38d0b450aad4) with energies  E 1 , 2 , {\displaystyle E_{1,2},} ![{\\displaystyle E_{1,2},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0c9861f0dab9853b22cb5b6296389f1c98e5096d) superimposed to create a composite state

     Ψ ( x , t ) = a ψ 1 ( x ) e − i E 1 t / h + b ψ 2 ( x ) e − i E 2 t / h . {\displaystyle \Psi (x,t)=a\psi _{1}(x)e^{-iE_{1}t/h}+b\psi _{2}(x)e^{-iE_{2}t/h}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\Psi \(x,t\)=a\\psi _{1}\(x\)e^{-iE_{1}t/h}+b\\psi _{2}\(x\)e^{-iE_{2}t/h}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9fd099d37e6bca142f1502c6340983946fa70c08)     The [probability amplitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probability_amplitude "Probability amplitude") of this state has a time-dependent interference term:      | Ψ ( x , t ) | 2 = a 2 | ψ 1 ( x ) | 2 + b 2 | ψ 2 ( x ) | 2 + 2 a b cos ⁡ ( E 2 − E 1 ℏ t ) . {\displaystyle |\Psi (x,t)|^{2}=a^{2}|\psi _{1}(x)|^{2}+b^{2}|\psi _{2}(x)|^{2}+2ab\cos({\frac {E_{2}-E_{1}}{\hbar }}t).} ![{\\displaystyle |\\Psi \(x,t\)|^{2}=a^{2}|\\psi _{1}\(x\)|^{2}+b^{2}|\\psi _{2}\(x\)|^{2}+2ab\\cos\({\\frac {E_{2}-E_{1}}{\\hbar }}t\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b443e0350b8eebcd9db41db61e4b50a16f0149a1)     The oscillation period varies inversely with the energy difference:  τ = 2 π ℏ / ( E 2 − E 1 ) {\displaystyle \tau =2\pi \hbar /(E_{2}-E_{1})} ![{\\displaystyle \\tau =2\\pi \\hbar /\(E_{2}-E_{1}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/209f3dd42e4e3b91e0255e3a7dd3004cac4c631d).[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GriffithsSchroeter2018-17): 144 
Each example has a different meaning for the time uncertainty, according to the observable and state used. 
### Quantum field theory
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=20 "Edit section: Quantum field theory")]
Some formulations of [quantum field theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_field_theory "Quantum field theory") uses temporary electron–positron pairs in its calculations called [virtual particles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtual_particles "Virtual particles"). The mass-energy and lifetime of these particles are related by the energy–time uncertainty relation. The energy of a quantum systems is not known with enough precision to limit their behavior to a single, simple history. Thus the influence of _all histories_ must be incorporated into quantum calculations, including those with much greater or much less energy than the mean of the measured/calculated energy distribution. 
The energy–time uncertainty principle does not temporarily violate [conservation of energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_of_energy "Conservation of energy"); it does not imply that energy can be "borrowed" from the universe as long as it is "returned" within a short amount of time.[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GriffithsSchroeter2018-17): 145  The energy of the universe is not an exactly known parameter at all times.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1) When events transpire at very short time intervals, there is uncertainty in the energy of these events. 
## Harmonic analysis
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=21 "Edit section: Harmonic analysis")]
Main article: [Fourier transform § Uncertainty principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourier_transform#Uncertainty_principle "Fourier transform")
In the context of [harmonic analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmonic_analysis "Harmonic analysis") the uncertainty principle implies that one cannot at the same time localize the value of a function and its Fourier transform. To wit, the following inequality holds,  ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ x 2 | f ( x ) | 2 d x ) ( ∫ − ∞ ∞ ξ 2 | f ^ ( ξ ) | 2 d ξ ) ≥ ‖ f ‖ 2 4 16 π 2 . {\displaystyle \left(\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }x^{2}|f(x)|^{2}\,dx\right)\left(\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }\xi ^{2}|{\hat {f}}(\xi )|^{2}\,d\xi \right)\geq {\frac {\|f\|_{2}^{4}}{16\pi ^{2}}}.} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\(\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }x^{2}|f\(x\)|^{2}\\,dx\\right\)\\left\(\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\xi ^{2}|{\\hat {f}}\(\\xi \)|^{2}\\,d\\xi \\right\)\\geq {\\frac {\\|f\\|_{2}^{4}}{16\\pi ^{2}}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4063c1b7f76d1ee5272dae316a5c23724961ed99)
Further mathematical uncertainty inequalities, including the above [entropic uncertainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropic_uncertainty "Entropic uncertainty"), hold between a function f and its Fourier transform  ƒ̂:[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-49)[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-50)[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-51) H x + H ξ ≥ log ⁡ ( e / 2 ) {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{\xi }\geq \log(e/2)} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{\\xi }\\geq \\log\(e/2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ceab027973e5b663ea22dd5a6c5ee6790ffd710c)
### Signal processing 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=22 "Edit section: Signal processing")]
In the context of [time–frequency analysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time%E2%80%93frequency_analysis "Time–frequency analysis") uncertainty principles are referred to as the **Gabor limit** , after [Dennis Gabor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dennis_Gabor "Dennis Gabor"), or sometimes the _Heisenberg–Gabor limit_. The basic result, which follows from "Benedicks's theorem", below, is that a function cannot be both [time limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_limited "Time limited") and [band limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Band_limited "Band limited") (a function and its Fourier transform cannot both have bounded domain)—see [bandlimited versus timelimited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandlimiting#Bandlimited_versus_timelimited "Bandlimiting"). More accurately, the _time-bandwidth_ or _duration-bandwidth_ product satisfies  σ t σ f ≥ 1 4 π ≈ 0.08 cycles , {\displaystyle \sigma _{t}\sigma _{f}\geq {\frac {1}{4\pi }}\approx 0.08{\text{ cycles}},} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{t}\\sigma _{f}\\geq {\\frac {1}{4\\pi }}\\approx 0.08{\\text{ cycles}},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/46aa8238211dd0c4e27b151b6d878e8754ff0c60) where  σ t {\displaystyle \sigma _{t}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{t}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b5dd6db0238ac32f34c6feb604748e253e842356) and  σ f {\displaystyle \sigma _{f}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{f}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1faac91a4f954c5707a88c07bfed70b9b92b3785) are the standard deviations of the time and frequency energy concentrations respectively.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-52) The minimum is attained for a [Gaussian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaussian_function "Gaussian function")-shaped pulse ([Gabor wavelet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabor_wavelet "Gabor wavelet")) [For the un-squared Gaussian (i.e. signal amplitude) and its un-squared Fourier transform magnitude  σ t σ f = 1 / 2 π {\displaystyle \sigma _{t}\sigma _{f}=1/2\pi } ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{t}\\sigma _{f}=1/2\\pi }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/661ed224f9633f4d1bdc61822aa52b6922e88834); squaring reduces each  σ {\displaystyle \sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/59f59b7c3e6fdb1d0365a494b81fb9a696138c36) by a factor  2 {\displaystyle {\sqrt {2}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\sqrt {2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b4afc1e27d418021bf10898eb44a7f5f315735ff).] Another common measure is the product of the time and frequency [full width at half maximum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_width_at_half_maximum "Full width at half maximum") (of the power/energy), which for the Gaussian equals  2 ln ⁡ 2 / π ≈ 0.44 {\displaystyle 2\ln 2/\pi \approx 0.44} ![{\\displaystyle 2\\ln 2/\\pi \\approx 0.44}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6961053891ef77849616f304d16b648dcb8723a2) (see [bandwidth-limited pulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandwidth-limited_pulse "Bandwidth-limited pulse")). 
Stated differently, one cannot simultaneously sharply localize a signal f in both the [time domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_domain "Time domain") and [frequency domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frequency_domain "Frequency domain"). 
When applied to [filters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_\(signal_processing\) "Filter \(signal processing\)"), the result implies that one cannot simultaneously achieve a high temporal resolution and high frequency resolution at the same time; a concrete example are the [resolution issues of the short-time Fourier transform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-time_Fourier_transform#Resolution_issues "Short-time Fourier transform")—if one uses a wide window, one achieves good frequency resolution at the cost of temporal resolution, while a narrow window has the opposite trade-off. 
Alternate theorems give more precise quantitative results, and, in time–frequency analysis, rather than interpreting the (1-dimensional) time and frequency domains separately, one instead interprets the limit as a lower limit on the support of a function in the (2-dimensional) time–frequency plane. In practice, the Gabor limit limits the _simultaneous_ time–frequency resolution one can achieve without interference; it is possible to achieve higher resolution, but at the cost of different components of the signal interfering with each other. 
As a result, in order to analyze signals where the [transients](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transient_\(acoustics\) "Transient \(acoustics\)") are important, the [wavelet transform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavelet_Transform "Wavelet Transform") is often used instead of the Fourier. 
### Discrete Fourier transform
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=23 "Edit section: Discrete Fourier transform")]
Let  { x n } := x 0 , x 1 , … , x N − 1 {\displaystyle \left\\{\mathbf {x_{n}} \right\\}:=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots ,x_{N-1}} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\\{\\mathbf {x_{n}} \\right\\}:=x_{0},x_{1},\\ldots ,x_{N-1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7bc5aaef9d6271db7cbc8511087b46768427fe1a) be a sequence of _N_ complex numbers and  { X k } := X 0 , X 1 , … , X N − 1 , {\displaystyle \left\\{\mathbf {X_{k}} \right\\}:=X_{0},X_{1},\ldots ,X_{N-1},} ![{\\displaystyle \\left\\{\\mathbf {X_{k}} \\right\\}:=X_{0},X_{1},\\ldots ,X_{N-1},}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0f1ca1e33e5cbb4f5c25b15cbc56c1649a79714f) be its [ discrete Fourier transform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrete_Fourier_transform#Uncertainty_principles "Discrete Fourier transform"). 
Denote by  ‖ x ‖ 0 {\displaystyle \|x\|_{0}} ![{\\displaystyle \\|x\\|_{0}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7a3901b541e26a3034cfeeb8c4fa4305b9c9d272) the number of non-zero elements in the time sequence  x 0 , x 1 , … , x N − 1 {\displaystyle x_{0},x_{1},\ldots ,x_{N-1}} ![{\\displaystyle x_{0},x_{1},\\ldots ,x_{N-1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2be07b5907424591389854129bb8f9125523fe5b) and by  ‖ X ‖ 0 {\displaystyle \|X\|_{0}} ![{\\displaystyle \\|X\\|_{0}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b28de5c75fe3a4f820ccee6ea02124c9eaed64be) the number of non-zero elements in the frequency sequence  X 0 , X 1 , … , X N − 1 {\displaystyle X_{0},X_{1},\ldots ,X_{N-1}} ![{\\displaystyle X_{0},X_{1},\\ldots ,X_{N-1}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7f5d9f8c5de39dbb82070c05aa19461051be4a20). Then,  ‖ x ‖ 0 ⋅ ‖ X ‖ 0 ≥ N . {\displaystyle \|x\|_{0}\cdot \|X\|_{0}\geq N.} ![{\\displaystyle \\|x\\|_{0}\\cdot \\|X\\|_{0}\\geq N.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6c3d4d06dd055c27f8acb189abd92a660285cdb8)
This inequality is [sharp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inequality_\(mathematics\)#Sharp_inequalities "Inequality \(mathematics\)"), with equality achieved when _x_ or _X_ is a Dirac mass, or more generally when _x_ is a nonzero multiple of a Dirac comb supported on a subgroup of the integers modulo _N_ (in which case _X_ is also a Dirac comb supported on a complementary subgroup, and vice versa). 
More generally, if _T_ and _W_ are subsets of the integers modulo _N_ , let  L T , R W : ℓ 2 ( Z / N Z ) → ℓ 2 ( Z / N Z ) {\displaystyle L_{T},R_{W}:\ell ^{2}(\mathbb {Z} /N\mathbb {Z} )\to \ell ^{2}(\mathbb {Z} /N\mathbb {Z} )} ![{\\displaystyle L_{T},R_{W}:\\ell ^{2}\(\\mathbb {Z} /N\\mathbb {Z} \)\\to \\ell ^{2}\(\\mathbb {Z} /N\\mathbb {Z} \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/f787e8400faf768c8b3eaaa7e9fff4c9ce48b589) denote the time-limiting operator and [band-limiting operators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandlimiting "Bandlimiting"), respectively. Then  ‖ L T R W ‖ 2 ≤ | T | | W | | G | {\displaystyle \|L_{T}R_{W}\|^{2}\leq {\frac {|T||W|}{|G|}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\|L_{T}R_{W}\\|^{2}\\leq {\\frac {|T||W|}{|G|}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/46000e03656635611b62082561df25d4cad20d11) where the norm is the [operator norm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operator_norm "Operator norm") of operators on the Hilbert space  ℓ 2 ( Z / N Z ) {\displaystyle \ell ^{2}(\mathbb {Z} /N\mathbb {Z} )} ![{\\displaystyle \\ell ^{2}\(\\mathbb {Z} /N\\mathbb {Z} \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6b418747824d3c2971846a892890b21e39e1dcfb) of functions on the integers modulo _N_. This inequality has implications for [signal reconstruction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signal_reconstruction "Signal reconstruction").[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Donoho-53)
When _N_ is a [prime number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_number "Prime number"), a stronger inequality holds:  ‖ x ‖ 0 + ‖ X ‖ 0 ≥ N + 1. {\displaystyle \|x\|_{0}+\|X\|_{0}\geq N+1.} ![{\\displaystyle \\|x\\|_{0}+\\|X\\|_{0}\\geq N+1.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d0cbdf116b80ac55eb0403ae7c9e6d1be9d946cc) Discovered by [Terence Tao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terence_Tao "Terence Tao"), this inequality is also sharp.[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-54)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=24 "Edit section: Benedicks's theorem")]
Amrein–Berthier[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-55) and Benedicks's theorem[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-56) intuitively says that the set of points where f is non-zero and the set of points where ƒ̂ is non-zero cannot both be small. 
Specifically, it is impossible for a function f in _L_ 2(**R**) and its Fourier transform ƒ̂ to both be [supported](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Support_of_a_function "Support of a function") on sets of finite [Lebesgue measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebesgue_measure "Lebesgue measure"). A more quantitative version is[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-57)[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-58) ‖ f ‖ L 2 ( R d ) ≤ C e C | S | | Σ | ( ‖ f ‖ L 2 ( S c ) + ‖ f ^ ‖ L 2 ( Σ c ) ) . {\displaystyle \|f\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf {R} ^{d})}\leq Ce^{C|S||\Sigma |}{\bigl (}\|f\|_{L^{2}(S^{c})}+\|{\hat {f}}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma ^{c})}{\bigr )}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\|f\\|_{L^{2}\(\\mathbf {R} ^{d}\)}\\leq Ce^{C|S||\\Sigma |}{\\bigl \(}\\|f\\|_{L^{2}\(S^{c}\)}+\\|{\\hat {f}}\\|_{L^{2}\(\\Sigma ^{c}\)}{\\bigr \)}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8c53650f5cfebe9655b9af518cda83037d5ff799)
One expects that the factor _Ce_ _C_ |_S_ ||_Σ_ | may be replaced by _Ce_ _C_(|_S_ ||_Σ_ |)1/_d_ , which is only known if either S or Σ is convex. 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=25 "Edit section: Hardy's uncertainty principle")]
The mathematician [G. H. Hardy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G._H._Hardy "G. H. Hardy") formulated the following uncertainty principle:[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-59) it is not possible for f and  ƒ̂ to both be "very rapidly decreasing". Specifically, if f in  L 2 ( R ) {\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb {R} )} ![{\\displaystyle L^{2}\(\\mathbb {R} \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8722fb232f689925a4baa0e4ba478e43ee346672) is such that  | f ( x ) | ≤ C ( 1 + | x | ) N e − a π x 2 {\displaystyle |f(x)|\leq C(1+|x|)^{N}e^{-a\pi x^{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle |f\(x\)|\\leq C\(1+|x|\)^{N}e^{-a\\pi x^{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/54a3874c0a7218f4e12d47f6afa3b4aa5f73b708) and  | f ^ ( ξ ) | ≤ C ( 1 + | ξ | ) N e − b π ξ 2 {\displaystyle |{\hat {f}}(\xi )|\leq C(1+|\xi |)^{N}e^{-b\pi \xi ^{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle |{\\hat {f}}\(\\xi \)|\\leq C\(1+|\\xi |\)^{N}e^{-b\\pi \\xi ^{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d1fc3ec2e6baf0d90ce026788b2c493fc01ad253) ( C > 0 , N {\displaystyle C>0,N} ![{\\displaystyle C>0,N}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3bd15c1f755778480125f8aee37cd9b1f91e4178) an integer), then, if _ab_ > 1, _f_ = 0, while if _ab_ = 1, then there is a polynomial P of degree ≤ _N_ such that  f ( x ) = P ( x ) e − a π x 2 . {\displaystyle f(x)=P(x)e^{-a\pi x^{2}}.} ![{\\displaystyle f\(x\)=P\(x\)e^{-a\\pi x^{2}}.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/eef8515b3cc4b9a8db96ed86632bc8beb24ca0a5)
This was later improved as follows: if  f ∈ L 2 ( R d ) {\displaystyle f\in L^{2}(\mathbb {R} ^{d})} ![{\\displaystyle f\\in L^{2}\(\\mathbb {R} ^{d}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/949f5203c6d5d0950764e98a8c095d8d55f8fd02) is such that  ∫ R d ∫ R d | f ( x ) | | f ^ ( ξ ) | e π | ⟨ x , ξ ⟩ | ( 1 + | x | + | ξ | ) N d x d ξ < + ∞ , {\displaystyle \int _{\mathbb {R} ^{d}}\int _{\mathbb {R} ^{d}}|f(x)||{\hat {f}}(\xi )|{\frac {e^{\pi |\langle x,\xi \rangle |}}{(1+|x|+|\xi |)^{N}}}\,dx\,d\xi <+\infty ~,} ![{\\displaystyle \\int _{\\mathbb {R} ^{d}}\\int _{\\mathbb {R} ^{d}}|f\(x\)||{\\hat {f}}\(\\xi \)|{\\frac {e^{\\pi |\\langle x,\\xi \\rangle |}}{\(1+|x|+|\\xi |\)^{N}}}\\,dx\\,d\\xi <+\\infty ~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1b842a3524ba017c297d17e1c96ed0175316346d) then  f ( x ) = P ( x ) e − π ⟨ A x , x ⟩ , {\displaystyle f(x)=P(x)e^{-\pi \langle Ax,x\rangle }~,} ![{\\displaystyle f\(x\)=P\(x\)e^{-\\pi \\langle Ax,x\\rangle }~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b862b7a922260a4302d91753e095c33a54007277) where P is a polynomial of degree (_N_ − _d_)/2 and A is a real _d_ × _d_ positive definite matrix. 
This result was stated in Beurling's complete works without proof and proved in Hörmander[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-60) (the case  d = 1 , N = 0 {\displaystyle d=1,N=0} ![{\\displaystyle d=1,N=0}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2a42acb4983595fe2b76bdf7b601b141c937a5d4)) and Bonami, Demange, and Jaming[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-61) for the general case. Note that Hörmander–Beurling's version implies the case _ab_ > 1 in Hardy's Theorem while the version by Bonami–Demange–Jaming covers the full strength of Hardy's Theorem. A different proof of Beurling's theorem based on Liouville's theorem appeared in ref.[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-62)
A full description of the case _ab_ < 1 as well as the following extension to Schwartz class distributions appears in ref.[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-63)
**Theorem** — If a tempered distribution  f ∈ S ′ ( R d ) {\displaystyle f\in {\mathcal {S}}'(\mathbb {R} ^{d})} ![{\\displaystyle f\\in {\\mathcal {S}}'\(\\mathbb {R} ^{d}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/43dde97c78f5e10080aa254b633dd49f57c32034) is such that  e π | x | 2 f ∈ S ′ ( R d ) {\displaystyle e^{\pi |x|^{2}}f\in {\mathcal {S}}'(\mathbb {R} ^{d})} ![{\\displaystyle e^{\\pi |x|^{2}}f\\in {\\mathcal {S}}'\(\\mathbb {R} ^{d}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/0edfee223fd0c87ef724859705c39d9c56de6ae2) and  e π | ξ | 2 f ^ ∈ S ′ ( R d ) , {\displaystyle e^{\pi |\xi |^{2}}{\hat {f}}\in {\mathcal {S}}'(\mathbb {R} ^{d})~,} ![{\\displaystyle e^{\\pi |\\xi |^{2}}{\\hat {f}}\\in {\\mathcal {S}}'\(\\mathbb {R} ^{d}\)~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3cf9fd69d658943110610167b246f6cc6ba04f67) then  f ( x ) = P ( x ) e − π ⟨ A x , x ⟩ , {\displaystyle f(x)=P(x)e^{-\pi \langle Ax,x\rangle }~,} ![{\\displaystyle f\(x\)=P\(x\)e^{-\\pi \\langle Ax,x\\rangle }~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b862b7a922260a4302d91753e095c33a54007277) for some convenient polynomial P and real positive definite matrix A of type _d_ × _d_. 
## Additional uncertainty relations
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=26 "Edit section: Additional uncertainty relations")]
### Heisenberg limit
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=27 "Edit section: Heisenberg limit")]
In [quantum metrology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_metrology "Quantum metrology"), and especially [interferometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interferometry "Interferometry"), the **Heisenberg limit** is the optimal rate at which the accuracy of a measurement can scale with the energy used in the measurement. Typically, this is the measurement of a phase (applied to one arm of a [beam-splitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beam-splitter "Beam-splitter")) and the energy is given by the number of photons used in an [interferometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interferometer "Interferometer"). Although some claim to have broken the Heisenberg limit, this reflects disagreement on the definition of the scaling resource.[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-64) Suitably defined, the Heisenberg limit is a consequence of the basic principles of quantum mechanics and cannot be beaten, although the weak Heisenberg limit can be beaten.[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-65)
### Systematic and statistical errors
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=28 "Edit section: Systematic and statistical errors")]
The inequalities above focus on the _statistical imprecision_ of observables as quantified by the standard deviation  σ {\displaystyle \sigma } ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/59f59b7c3e6fdb1d0365a494b81fb9a696138c36). Heisenberg's original version, however, was dealing with the _systematic error_ , a disturbance of the quantum system produced by the measuring apparatus, i.e., an observer effect. 
If we let  ε A {\displaystyle \varepsilon _{A}} ![{\\displaystyle \\varepsilon _{A}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3be1275ab1253a02db130d8b3e9183ba060ad27f) represent the error (i.e., [inaccuracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accuracy "Accuracy")) of a measurement of an observable _A_ and  η B {\displaystyle \eta _{B}} ![{\\displaystyle \\eta _{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1ae72a0e6af33a73ce4e6ae94ac029135f582379) the disturbance produced on a subsequent measurement of the conjugate variable _B_ by the former measurement of _A_ , then the inequality proposed by Masanao Ozawa − encompassing both systematic and statistical errors - holds:[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Ozawa2003-66)
ε A η B + ε A σ B + σ A η B ≥ 1 2 | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | {\displaystyle \varepsilon _{A}\,\eta _{B}+\varepsilon _{A}\,\sigma _{B}+\sigma _{A}\,\eta _{B}\,\geq \,{\frac {1}{2}}\,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|} ![{\\displaystyle \\varepsilon _{A}\\,\\eta _{B}+\\varepsilon _{A}\\,\\sigma _{B}+\\sigma _{A}\\,\\eta _{B}\\,\\geq \\,{\\frac {1}{2}}\\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e07b031a15d10a6380f7ee47da5c5c1dfb93e31a)
Heisenberg's uncertainty principle, as originally described in the 1927 formulation, mentions only the first term of Ozawa inequality, regarding the _systematic error_. Using the notation above to describe the _error/disturbance_ effect of _sequential measurements_ (first _A_ , then _B_), it could be written as 
ε A η B ≥ 1 2 | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | {\displaystyle \varepsilon _{A}\,\eta _{B}\,\geq \,{\frac {1}{2}}\,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|} ![{\\displaystyle \\varepsilon _{A}\\,\\eta _{B}\\,\\geq \\,{\\frac {1}{2}}\\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/28d08057de34306115c996b9943fb0603282befc)
The formal derivation of the Heisenberg relation is possible but far from intuitive. It was _not_ proposed by Heisenberg, but formulated in a mathematically consistent way only in recent years.[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-67)[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-68) Also, it must be stressed that the Heisenberg formulation is not taking into account the intrinsic statistical errors  σ A {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/399e9d09fb7fc7fc1f286c97d3bde5ef6a814fbe) and  σ B {\displaystyle \sigma _{B}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{B}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6daf3fafcc7df4a3fda4f23f5c09b9c3944c346f). There is increasing experimental evidence[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Rozema-69)[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-70)[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-71)[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-72) that the total quantum uncertainty cannot be described by the Heisenberg term alone, but requires the presence of all the three terms of the Ozawa inequality. 
Using the same formalism,[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1) it is also possible to introduce the other kind of physical situation, often confused with the previous one, namely the case of _simultaneous measurements_ (_A_ and _B_ at the same time): 
ε A ε B ≥ 1 2 | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | {\displaystyle \varepsilon _{A}\,\varepsilon _{B}\,\geq \,{\frac {1}{2}}\,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|} ![{\\displaystyle \\varepsilon _{A}\\,\\varepsilon _{B}\\,\\geq \\,{\\frac {1}{2}}\\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8f93231686a00ca50485a5eccdadddfbc4f6b2c3)
The two simultaneous measurements on _A_ and _B_ are necessarily[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-73) _unsharp_ or [_weak_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weak_measurement "Weak measurement"). 
It is also possible to derive an uncertainty relation that, as the Ozawa's one, combines both the statistical and systematic error components, but keeps a form very close to the Heisenberg original inequality. By adding Robertson[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1)
σ A σ B ≥ 1 2 | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | {\displaystyle \sigma _{A}\,\sigma _{B}\,\geq \,{\frac {1}{2}}\,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{A}\\,\\sigma _{B}\\,\\geq \\,{\\frac {1}{2}}\\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/1606cabaad53903e8110417fff05ebda20a2dd41)
and Ozawa relations we obtain  ε A η B + ε A σ B + σ A η B + σ A σ B ≥ | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | . {\displaystyle \varepsilon _{A}\eta _{B}+\varepsilon _{A}\,\sigma _{B}+\sigma _{A}\,\eta _{B}+\sigma _{A}\sigma _{B}\geq \left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|.} ![{\\displaystyle \\varepsilon _{A}\\eta _{B}+\\varepsilon _{A}\\,\\sigma _{B}+\\sigma _{A}\\,\\eta _{B}+\\sigma _{A}\\sigma _{B}\\geq \\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/99801a6fd6e9d9f36223cce7aba8a30cf39e6230) The four terms can be written as:  ( ε A + σ A ) ( η B + σ B ) ≥ | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | . {\displaystyle (\varepsilon _{A}+\sigma _{A})\,(\eta _{B}+\sigma _{B})\,\geq \,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|.} ![{\\displaystyle \(\\varepsilon _{A}+\\sigma _{A}\)\\,\(\\eta _{B}+\\sigma _{B}\)\\,\\geq \\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/8ec1acc4af7b675c838b09fc719057c5d16819c4) Defining:  ε ¯ A ≡ ( ε A + σ A ) {\displaystyle {\bar {\varepsilon }}_{A}\,\equiv \,(\varepsilon _{A}+\sigma _{A})} ![{\\displaystyle {\\bar {\\varepsilon }}_{A}\\,\\equiv \\,\(\\varepsilon _{A}+\\sigma _{A}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ab7202c4719ce275336a74dceb9cab2ebd1ce2ef) as the _inaccuracy_ in the measured values of the variable _A_ and  η ¯ B ≡ ( η B + σ B ) {\displaystyle {\bar {\eta }}_{B}\,\equiv \,(\eta _{B}+\sigma _{B})} ![{\\displaystyle {\\bar {\\eta }}_{B}\\,\\equiv \\,\(\\eta _{B}+\\sigma _{B}\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/62dcd34a6fc4c49d553b9075071015021cd43e73) as the _resulting fluctuation_ in the conjugate variable _B_ , Kazuo Fujikawa[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-74) established an uncertainty relation similar to the Heisenberg original one, but valid both for _systematic and statistical errors_ : 
ε ¯ A η ¯ B ≥ | ⟨ [ A ^ , B ^ ] ⟩ | {\displaystyle {\bar {\varepsilon }}_{A}\,{\bar {\eta }}_{B}\,\geq \,\left|{\Bigl \langle }{\bigl [}{\hat {A}},{\hat {B}}{\bigr ]}{\Bigr \rangle }\right|} ![{\\displaystyle {\\bar {\\varepsilon }}_{A}\\,{\\bar {\\eta }}_{B}\\,\\geq \\,\\left|{\\Bigl \\langle }{\\bigl \[}{\\hat {A}},{\\hat {B}}{\\bigr \]}{\\Bigr \\rangle }\\right|}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bb507c6e7fa92a94bfb07f979999ccbb5ac7450a)
### Quantum entropic uncertainty principle
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=29 "Edit section: Quantum entropic uncertainty principle")]
For many distributions, the standard deviation is not a particularly natural way of quantifying the structure. For example, uncertainty relations in which one of the observables is an angle has little physical meaning for fluctuations larger than one period.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-CarruthersNieto-22)[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-75)[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-76)[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-77) Other examples include highly [bimodal distributions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bimodal_distribution "Bimodal distribution"), or [unimodal distributions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unimodal_distribution "Unimodal distribution") with divergent variance. 
A solution that overcomes these issues is an uncertainty based on [entropic uncertainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entropic_uncertainty "Entropic uncertainty") instead of the product of variances. While formulating the [many-worlds interpretation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Many-worlds_interpretation "Many-worlds interpretation") of quantum mechanics in 1957, [Hugh Everett III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Everett_III "Hugh Everett III") conjectured a stronger extension of the uncertainty principle based on entropic certainty.[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-78) This conjecture, also studied by I. I. Hirschman[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-79) and proven in 1975 by W. Beckner[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Beckner-80) and by Iwo Bialynicki-Birula and Jerzy Mycielski[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-BBM-81) is that, for two normalized, dimensionless Fourier transform pairs _f_(_a_) and _g_(_b_) where       f ( a ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ g ( b ) e 2 π i a b d b {\displaystyle f(a)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }g(b)\ e^{2\pi iab}\,db} ![{\\displaystyle f\(a\)=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }g\(b\)\\ e^{2\\pi iab}\\,db}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b46f8f6c892788d27ea80ea095423919ccc8b8c7) g ( b ) = ∫ − ∞ ∞ f ( a ) e − 2 π i a b d a {\displaystyle \,\,\,g(b)=\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }f(a)\ e^{-2\pi iab}\,da} ![{\\displaystyle \\,\\,\\,g\(b\)=\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }f\(a\)\\ e^{-2\\pi iab}\\,da}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/c44ab7dd81bcad69afb4cbfe1be793b316b0b36b)
