the Shannon [information entropies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_entropy "Information entropy") H a = − ∫ − ∞ ∞ | f ( a ) | 2 log ⁡ | f ( a ) | 2 d a , {\displaystyle H_{a}=-\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|f(a)|^{2}\log |f(a)|^{2}\,da,} ![{\\displaystyle H_{a}=-\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|f\(a\)|^{2}\\log |f\(a\)|^{2}\\,da,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/02728e5d732c3d780b1df580ee0ac568cb25a4f3) and  H b = − ∫ − ∞ ∞ | g ( b ) | 2 log ⁡ | g ( b ) | 2 d b {\displaystyle H_{b}=-\int _{-\infty }^{\infty }|g(b)|^{2}\log |g(b)|^{2}\,db} ![{\\displaystyle H_{b}=-\\int _{-\\infty }^{\\infty }|g\(b\)|^{2}\\log |g\(b\)|^{2}\\,db}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/547f5dfae580d9606e4ed7f1ffbe5a5c3318623c) are subject to the following constraint, 
H a + H b ≥ log ⁡ ( e / 2 ) {\displaystyle H_{a}+H_{b}\geq \log(e/2)} ![{\\displaystyle H_{a}+H_{b}\\geq \\log\(e/2\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3e443401a1a6387387a18fc3e6f003775cb6d13f)
where the logarithms may be in any base. 
The probability distribution functions associated with the position wave function _ψ_(_x_) and the momentum wave function _φ_(_x_) have dimensions of inverse length and momentum respectively, but the entropies may be rendered dimensionless by  H x = − ∫ | ψ ( x ) | 2 ln ⁡ ( x 0 | ψ ( x ) | 2 ) d x = − ⟨ ln ⁡ ( x 0 | ψ ( x ) | 2 ) ⟩ {\displaystyle H_{x}=-\int |\psi (x)|^{2}\ln \left(x_{0}\,|\psi (x)|^{2}\right)dx=-\left\langle \ln \left(x_{0}\,\left|\psi (x)\right|^{2}\right)\right\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}=-\\int |\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\ln \\left\(x_{0}\\,|\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\right\)dx=-\\left\\langle \\ln \\left\(x_{0}\\,\\left|\\psi \(x\)\\right|^{2}\\right\)\\right\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b808c227a7edbee778050a742e4d41cf318dd895) H p = − ∫ | φ ( p ) | 2 ln ⁡ ( p 0 | φ ( p ) | 2 ) d p = − ⟨ ln ⁡ ( p 0 | φ ( p ) | 2 ) ⟩ {\displaystyle H_{p}=-\int |\varphi (p)|^{2}\ln(p_{0}\,|\varphi (p)|^{2})\,dp=-\left\langle \ln(p_{0}\left|\varphi (p)\right|^{2})\right\rangle } ![{\\displaystyle H_{p}=-\\int |\\varphi \(p\)|^{2}\\ln\(p_{0}\\,|\\varphi \(p\)|^{2}\)\\,dp=-\\left\\langle \\ln\(p_{0}\\left|\\varphi \(p\)\\right|^{2}\)\\right\\rangle }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/30048a1d62622b5904b217575f99893cf0280f99) where _x_ 0 and _p_ 0 are some arbitrarily chosen length and momentum respectively, which render the arguments of the logarithms dimensionless. Note that the entropies will be functions of these chosen parameters. Due to the [Fourier transform relation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavefunction#Relation_between_wave_functions "Wavefunction") between the position wave function _ψ_(_x_) and the momentum wavefunction _φ_(_p_), the above constraint can be written for the corresponding entropies as 
H x + H p ≥ log ⁡ ( e h 2 x 0 p 0 ) {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\geq \log \left({\frac {e\,h}{2\,x_{0}\,p_{0}}}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\geq \\log \\left\({\\frac {e\\,h}{2\\,x_{0}\\,p_{0}}}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e34ddbcccfafd950fabdfe72d56940486d404c5a)
where h is the [Planck constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_constant "Planck constant"). 
Depending on one's choice of the _x 0 p0_ product, the expression may be written in many ways. If _x_ 0 _p_ 0 is chosen to be h, then  H x + H p ≥ log ⁡ ( e 2 ) {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\geq \log \left({\frac {e}{2}}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\geq \\log \\left\({\\frac {e}{2}}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b8fbe8c0fe65cb36f1c6bce20f683e7323108aef)
If, instead, _x_ 0 _p_ 0 is chosen to be  ℏ {\displaystyle \hbar } ![{\\displaystyle \\hbar }](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/de68de3a92517953436c93b5a76461d49160cc41), then  H x + H p ≥ log ⁡ ( e π ) {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\geq \log(e\,\pi )} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\geq \\log\(e\\,\\pi \)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/30da148e2a9e0129f5f07de8e46bd33024563c09)
If _x_ 0 and _p_ 0 are chosen to be unity in whatever system of units are being used, then  H x + H p ≥ log ⁡ ( e h 2 ) {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\geq \log \left({\frac {e\,h}{2}}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\geq \\log \\left\({\\frac {e\\,h}{2}}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/640cd7190c45bbc462bfed96f8ee00e40b204c6e) where h is interpreted as a dimensionless number equal to the value of the Planck constant in the chosen system of units. Note that these inequalities can be extended to multimode quantum states, or wavefunctions in more than one spatial dimension.[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-82)
The quantum entropic uncertainty principle is more restrictive than the Heisenberg uncertainty principle. From the inverse [logarithmic Sobolev inequalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logarithmic_Sobolev_inequalities "Logarithmic Sobolev inequalities")[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-83) H x ≤ 1 2 log ⁡ ( 2 e π σ x 2 / x 0 2 ) , {\displaystyle H_{x}\leq {\frac {1}{2}}\log(2e\pi \sigma _{x}^{2}/x_{0}^{2})~,} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}\\leq {\\frac {1}{2}}\\log\(2e\\pi \\sigma _{x}^{2}/x_{0}^{2}\)~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/cfe4edf40695aad2b7289e1ac40d1fac6171ebba) H p ≤ 1 2 log ⁡ ( 2 e π σ p 2 / p 0 2 ) , {\displaystyle H_{p}\leq {\frac {1}{2}}\log(2e\pi \sigma _{p}^{2}/p_{0}^{2})~,} ![{\\displaystyle H_{p}\\leq {\\frac {1}{2}}\\log\(2e\\pi \\sigma _{p}^{2}/p_{0}^{2}\)~,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/9ed09815da333aec97299c238f49b2741bd3d633) (equivalently, from the fact that normal distributions maximize the entropy of all such with a given variance), it readily follows that this entropic uncertainty principle is _stronger than the one based on standard deviations_ , because  σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 2 exp ⁡ ( H x + H p − log ⁡ ( e h 2 x 0 p 0 ) ) ≥ ℏ 2 . {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}\exp \left(H_{x}+H_{p}-\log \left({\frac {e\,h}{2\,x_{0}\,p_{0}}}\right)\right)\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}~.} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}\\exp \\left\(H_{x}+H_{p}-\\log \\left\({\\frac {e\\,h}{2\\,x_{0}\\,p_{0}}}\\right\)\\right\)\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}~.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/213a0a54185f910edcca29d8343a769bf3f8e5b0)
In other words, the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, is a consequence of the quantum entropic uncertainty principle, but not vice versa. A few remarks on these inequalities. First, the choice of [base e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Base_e "Base e") is a matter of popular convention in physics. The logarithm can alternatively be in any base, provided that it be consistent on both sides of the inequality. Second, recall the [Shannon entropy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shannon_entropy "Shannon entropy") has been used, _not_ the quantum [von Neumann entropy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Neumann_entropy "Von Neumann entropy"). Finally, the normal distribution saturates the inequality, and it is the only distribution with this property, because it is the [maximum entropy probability distribution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximum_entropy_probability_distribution "Maximum entropy probability distribution") among those with fixed variance (cf. [here](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Differential_entropy#Maximization_in_the_normal_distribution "Differential entropy") for proof). 
showEntropic uncertainty of the normal distribution

value  H x + H p = ln ⁡ ( 2 π ) + 1 2 + ln ⁡ ( π 2 ) + 1 2 = 1 + ln ⁡ π = ln ⁡ ( e π ) . {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}H_{x}+H_{p}&=\ln({\sqrt {2\pi }})+{\frac {1}{2}}+\ln \left({\sqrt {\frac {\pi }{2}}}\\right\)+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\\\&=1+\\ln \\pi =\\ln\(e\\pi \).\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d699c594c124d1460cbe96cf37443f2ff03dc4ea)  
A measurement apparatus will have a finite resolution set by the discretization of its possible outputs into bins, with the probability of lying within one of the bins given by the Born rule. We will consider the most common experimental situation, in which the bins are of uniform size. Let _δx_ be a measure of the spatial resolution. We take the zeroth bin to be centered near the origin, with possibly some small constant offset _c_. The probability of lying within the jth interval of width _δx_ is  P ⁡ [ x j ] = ∫ ( j − 1 / 2 ) δ x − c ( j + 1 / 2 ) δ x − c | ψ ( x ) | 2 d x {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [x_{j}]=\int _{(j-1/2)\delta x-c}^{(j+1/2)\delta x-c}|\psi (x)|^{2}\,dx} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]=\\int _{\(j-1/2\)\\delta x-c}^{\(j+1/2\)\\delta x-c}|\\psi \(x\)|^{2}\\,dx}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b7c173529c81593fd8c2e56a4e1af15f369c365c)
To account for this discretization, we can define the Shannon entropy of the wave function for a given measurement apparatus as  H x = − ∑ j = − ∞ ∞ P ⁡ [ x j ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ x j ] . {\displaystyle H_{x}=-\sum _{j=-\infty }^{\infty }\operatorname {P} [x_{j}]\ln \operatorname {P} [x_{j}].} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}=-\\sum _{j=-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\].}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/25c2fd9ede05e0615e6eff1a8f2ace626debd818)
Under the above definition, the entropic uncertainty relation is  H x + H p > ln ⁡ ( e 2 ) − ln ⁡ ( δ x δ p h ) . {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}>\ln \left({\frac {e}{2}}\right)-\ln \left({\frac {\delta x\delta p}{h}}\right).} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}>\\ln \\left\({\\frac {e}{2}}\\right\)-\\ln \\left\({\\frac {\\delta x\\delta p}{h}}\\right\).}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/246dbdfcb1fe677ba62756deee9af8eaaf6faa09)
Here we note that _δx_ _δp_ /_h_ is a typical infinitesimal phase space volume used in the calculation of a [partition function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partition_function_\(statistical_mechanics\) "Partition function \(statistical mechanics\)"). The inequality is also strict and not saturated. Efforts to improve this bound are an active area of research. 
showNormal distribution example

We demonstrate this method first on the ground state of the QHO, which as discussed above saturates the usual uncertainty based on standard deviations.  ψ ( x ) = ( m ω π ℏ ) 1 / 4 exp ⁡ ( − m ω x 2 2 ℏ ) {\displaystyle \psi (x)=\left({\frac {m\omega }{\pi \hbar }}\right)^{1/4}\exp {\left(-{\frac {m\omega x^{2}}{2\hbar }}\right)}} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)=\\left\({\\frac {m\\omega }{\\pi \\hbar }}\\right\)^{1/4}\\exp {\\left\(-{\\frac {m\\omega x^{2}}{2\\hbar }}\\right\)}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/5854e875424d83707deca403649a809ee279dc50) The probability of lying within one of these bins can be expressed in terms of the [error function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Error_function "Error function").  P ⁡ [ x j ] = m ω π ℏ ∫ ( j − 1 / 2 ) δ x ( j + 1 / 2 ) δ x exp ⁡ ( − m ω x 2 ℏ ) d x = 1 π ∫ ( j − 1 / 2 ) δ x m ω / ℏ ( j + 1 / 2 ) δ x m ω / ℏ e u 2 d u = 1 2 [ erf ⁡ ( ( j + 1 2 ) δ x ⋅ m ω ℏ ) − erf ⁡ ( ( j − 1 2 ) δ x ⋅ m ω ℏ ) ] {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\operatorname {P} [x_{j}]&={\sqrt {\frac {m\omega }{\pi \hbar }}}\int _{(j-1/2)\delta x}^{(j+1/2)\delta x}\exp \left(-{\frac {m\omega x^{2}}{\hbar }}\right)\,dx\\\&={\sqrt {\frac {1}{\pi }}}\int _{(j-1/2)\delta x{\sqrt {m\omega /\hbar }}}^{(j+1/2)\delta x{\sqrt {m\omega /\hbar }}}e^{u^{2}}\,du\\\&={\frac {1}{2}}\left[\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j+{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\delta x\cdot {\sqrt {\frac {m\omega }{\hbar }}}\right)-\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j-{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\delta x\cdot {\sqrt {\frac {m\omega }{\hbar }}}\right)\right]\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}\\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]&={\\sqrt {\\frac {m\\omega }{\\pi \\hbar }}}\\int _{\(j-1/2\)\\delta x}^{\(j+1/2\)\\delta x}\\exp \\left\(-{\\frac {m\\omega x^{2}}{\\hbar }}\\right\)\\,dx\\\\&={\\sqrt {\\frac {1}{\\pi }}}\\int _{\(j-1/2\)\\delta x{\\sqrt {m\\omega /\\hbar }}}^{\(j+1/2\)\\delta x{\\sqrt {m\\omega /\\hbar }}}e^{u^{2}}\\,du\\\\&={\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\[\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\delta x\\cdot {\\sqrt {\\frac {m\\omega }{\\hbar }}}\\right\)-\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\delta x\\cdot {\\sqrt {\\frac {m\\omega }{\\hbar }}}\\right\)\\right\]\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fb98a69e46b2d10143459959d1679c78443b6378) The momentum probabilities are completely analogous.  P ⁡ [ p j ] = 1 2 [ erf ⁡ ( ( j + 1 2 ) δ p ⋅ 1 ℏ m ω ) − erf ⁡ ( ( j − 1 2 ) δ x ⋅ 1 ℏ m ω ) ] {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [p_{j}]={\frac {1}{2}}\left[\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j+{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\delta p\cdot {\frac {1}{\sqrt {\hbar m\omega }}}\right)-\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j-{\frac {1}{2}}\right)\delta x\cdot {\frac {1}{\sqrt {\hbar m\omega }}}\right)\right]} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]={\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\[\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\delta p\\cdot {\\frac {1}{\\sqrt {\\hbar m\\omega }}}\\right\)-\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\)\\delta x\\cdot {\\frac {1}{\\sqrt {\\hbar m\\omega }}}\\right\)\\right\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ed784fa900105bac03e1163a54fc3dd5732367aa) For simplicity, we will set the resolutions to  δ x = h m ω {\displaystyle \delta x={\sqrt {\frac {h}{m\omega }}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\delta x={\\sqrt {\\frac {h}{m\\omega }}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/704c292c1349fc88d626d37e73c1e460c1391365) − erf ⁡ ( ( j − 1 2 ) 2 π ) ] {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [x_{j}]=\operatorname {P} [p_{j}]={\frac {1}{2}}\left[\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j+{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)-\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j-{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)\right]} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]=\\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]={\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\[\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)-\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)\\right\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ba65e2143152330bf233c24e6524ac16a365c168) The Shannon entropy can be evaluated numerically.  H x = H p = − ∑ j = − ∞ ∞ P ⁡ [ x j ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ x j ] = − ∑ j = − ∞ ∞ 1 2 [ erf ⁡ ( ( j + 1 2 ) 2 π ) − erf ⁡ ( ( j − 1 2 ) 2 π ) ] ln ⁡ 1 2 [ erf ⁡ ( ( j + 1 2 ) 2 π ) − erf ⁡ ( ( j − 1 2 ) 2 π ) ] ≈ 0.3226 {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}H_{x}=H_{p}&=-\sum _{j=-\infty }^{\infty }\operatorname {P} [x_{j}]\ln \operatorname {P} [x_{j}]\\\&=-\sum _{j=-\infty }^{\infty }{\frac {1}{2}}\left[\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j+{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)-\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j-{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)\right]\ln {\frac {1}{2}}\left[\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j+{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)-\operatorname {erf} \left(\left(j-{\frac {1}{2}}\right){\sqrt {2\pi }}\right)\right]\\\&\approx 0.3226\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}H_{x}=H_{p}&=-\\sum _{j=-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]\\\\&=-\\sum _{j=-\\infty }^{\\infty }{\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\[\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)-\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)\\right\]\\ln {\\frac {1}{2}}\\left\[\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j+{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)-\\operatorname {erf} \\left\(\\left\(j-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\right\){\\sqrt {2\\pi }}\\right\)\\right\]\\\\&\\approx 0.3226\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ce13d4ff28b796ec3e3206a6d32b1c3eff5d693a) The entropic uncertainty is indeed larger than the limiting value.  H x + H p ≈ 0.3226 + 0.3226 = 0.6452 > ln ⁡ ( e 2 ) − ln ⁡ 1 ≈ 0.3069 {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\approx 0.3226+0.3226=0.6452>\ln \left({\frac {e}{2}}\right)-\ln 1\approx 0.3069} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\approx 0.3226+0.3226=0.6452>\\ln \\left\({\\frac {e}{2}}\\right\)-\\ln 1\\approx 0.3069}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/31a27b561bd82e9fd02f01af30d47953aecbaf9a) Note that despite being in the optimal case, the inequality is not saturated.   
showSinc function example   
---  
An example of a unimodal distribution with infinite variance is the [sinc function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinc_function "Sinc function"). If the wave function is the correctly normalized uniform distribution,  ψ ( x ) = { 1 / 2 a for  | x | ≤ a , 0 for  | x | > a {\displaystyle \psi (x)={\begin{cases}{1}/{\sqrt {2a}}&{\text{for }}|x|\leq a,\\\\[8pt]0&{\text{for }}|x|>a\end{cases}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\psi \(x\)={\\begin{cases}{1}/{\\sqrt {2a}}&{\\text{for }}|x|\\leq a,\\\\\[8pt\]0&{\\text{for }}|x|>a\\end{cases}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/6252d65b527d2b4bf3a708c339d7907976267c45) then its Fourier transform is the sinc function,  φ ( p ) = a π ℏ ⋅ sinc ⁡ ( a p ℏ ) {\displaystyle \varphi (p)={\sqrt {\frac {a}{\pi \hbar }}}\cdot \operatorname {sinc} \left({\frac {ap}{\hbar }}\right)} ![{\\displaystyle \\varphi \(p\)={\\sqrt {\\frac {a}{\\pi \\hbar }}}\\cdot \\operatorname {sinc} \\left\({\\frac {ap}{\\hbar }}\\right\)}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7e7c7ccc3a38f9d7ec07d0483d3b26b56d538d8f) which yields infinite momentum variance despite having a centralized shape. The entropic uncertainty, on the other hand, is finite. Suppose for simplicity that the spatial resolution is just a two-bin measurement, _δx_ =  _a_ , and that the momentum resolution is _δp_ =  _h_ /_a_.  Partitioning the uniform spatial distribution into two equal bins is straightforward. We set the offset _c_ = 1/2 so that the two bins span the distribution.  P ⁡ [ x 0 ] = ∫ − a 0 1 2 a d x = 1 2 {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [x_{0}]=\int _{-a}^{0}{\frac {1}{2a}}\,dx={\frac {1}{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[x_{0}\]=\\int _{-a}^{0}{\\frac {1}{2a}}\\,dx={\\frac {1}{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4bb8e8e07cba14e44ac3fd1f812c87b344f79978) P ⁡ [ x 1 ] = ∫ 0 a 1 2 a d x = 1 2 {\displaystyle \operatorname {P} [x_{1}]=\int _{0}^{a}{\frac {1}{2a}}\,dx={\frac {1}{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\operatorname {P} \[x_{1}\]=\\int _{0}^{a}{\\frac {1}{2a}}\\,dx={\\frac {1}{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/abb619286fac29cd04fb8ad539e4d7fc979da3fd) H x = − ∑ j = 0 1 P ⁡ [ x j ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ x j ] = − 1 2 ln ⁡ 1 2 − 1 2 ln ⁡ 1 2 = ln ⁡ 2 {\displaystyle H_{x}=-\sum _{j=0}^{1}\operatorname {P} [x_{j}]\ln \operatorname {P} [x_{j}]=-{\frac {1}{2}}\ln {\frac {1}{2}}-{\frac {1}{2}}\ln {\frac {1}{2}}=\ln 2} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}=-\\sum _{j=0}^{1}\\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[x_{j}\]=-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\ln {\\frac {1}{2}}-{\\frac {1}{2}}\\ln {\\frac {1}{2}}=\\ln 2}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/fd49a257fd5578026bd28e8e539dccfaae24dd1f) The bins for momentum must cover the entire real line. As done with the spatial distribution, we could apply an offset. It turns out, however, that the Shannon entropy is minimized when the zeroth bin for momentum is centered at the origin. (The reader is encouraged to try adding an offset.) The probability of lying within an arbitrary momentum bin can be expressed in terms of the [sine integral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sine_integral "Sine integral").  P ⁡ [ p j ] = a π ℏ ∫ ( j − 1 / 2 ) δ p ( j + 1 / 2 ) δ p sinc 2 ⁡ ( a p ℏ ) d p = 1 π ∫ 2 π ( j − 1 / 2 ) 2 π ( j + 1 / 2 ) sinc 2 ⁡ ( u ) d u = 1 π [ Si ⁡ ( ( 4 j + 2 ) π ) − Si ⁡ ( ( 4 j − 2 ) π ) ] {\displaystyle {\begin{aligned}\operatorname {P} [p_{j}]&={\frac {a}{\pi \hbar }}\int _{(j-1/2)\delta p}^{(j+1/2)\delta p}\operatorname {sinc} ^{2}\left({\frac {ap}{\hbar }}\right)\,dp\\\&={\frac {1}{\pi }}\int _{2\pi (j-1/2)}^{2\pi (j+1/2)}\operatorname {sinc} ^{2}(u)\,du\\\&={\frac {1}{\pi }}\left[\operatorname {Si} ((4j+2)\pi )-\operatorname {Si} ((4j-2)\pi )\right]\end{aligned}}} ![{\\displaystyle {\\begin{aligned}\\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]&={\\frac {a}{\\pi \\hbar }}\\int _{\(j-1/2\)\\delta p}^{\(j+1/2\)\\delta p}\\operatorname {sinc} ^{2}\\left\({\\frac {ap}{\\hbar }}\\right\)\\,dp\\\\&={\\frac {1}{\\pi }}\\int _{2\\pi \(j-1/2\)}^{2\\pi \(j+1/2\)}\\operatorname {sinc} ^{2}\(u\)\\,du\\\\&={\\frac {1}{\\pi }}\\left\[\\operatorname {Si} \(\(4j+2\)\\pi \)-\\operatorname {Si} \(\(4j-2\)\\pi \)\\right\]\\end{aligned}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/4c11b911bee6171d2a7ac1ee1c5522d49625ffb2) The Shannon entropy can be evaluated numerically.  H p = − ∑ j = − ∞ ∞ P ⁡ [ p j ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ p j ] = − P ⁡ [ p 0 ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ p 0 ] − 2 ⋅ ∑ j = 1 ∞ P ⁡ [ p j ] ln ⁡ P ⁡ [ p j ] ≈ 0.53 {\displaystyle H_{p}=-\sum _{j=-\infty }^{\infty }\operatorname {P} [p_{j}]\ln \operatorname {P} [p_{j}]=-\operatorname {P} [p_{0}]\ln \operatorname {P} [p_{0}]-2\cdot \sum _{j=1}^{\infty }\operatorname {P} [p_{j}]\ln \operatorname {P} [p_{j}]\approx 0.53} ![{\\displaystyle H_{p}=-\\sum _{j=-\\infty }^{\\infty }\\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]=-\\operatorname {P} \[p_{0}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[p_{0}\]-2\\cdot \\sum _{j=1}^{\\infty }\\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]\\ln \\operatorname {P} \[p_{j}\]\\approx 0.53}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/ae44da862dea854275b063fc3b5fe01bd3f3309f) The entropic uncertainty is indeed larger than the limiting value.  H x + H p ≈ 0.69 + 0.53 = 1.22 > ln ⁡ ( e 2 ) − ln ⁡ 1 ≈ 0.31 {\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\approx 0.69+0.53=1.22>\ln \left({\frac {e}{2}}\right)-\ln 1\approx 0.31} ![{\\displaystyle H_{x}+H_{p}\\approx 0.69+0.53=1.22>\\ln \\left\({\\frac {e}{2}}\\right\)-\\ln 1\\approx 0.31}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/979d41a023ebc6d3eae49cd97e6af99f96194743)  
### Uncertainty relation with three angular momentum components
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=30 "Edit section: Uncertainty relation with three angular momentum components")]
For a particle of [total angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_angular_momentum "Total angular momentum") j {\displaystyle j} ![{\\displaystyle j}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/2f461e54f5c093e92a55547b9764291390f0b5d0) the following uncertainty relation holds  σ J x 2 + σ J y 2 + σ J z 2 ≥ j , {\displaystyle \sigma _{J_{x}}^{2}+\sigma _{J_{y}}^{2}+\sigma _{J_{z}}^{2}\geq j,} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{J_{x}}^{2}+\\sigma _{J_{y}}^{2}+\\sigma _{J_{z}}^{2}\\geq j,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/bc7c391416de3f4bb77cbeaf7af9d639e47b210b) where  J l {\displaystyle J_{l}} ![{\\displaystyle J_{l}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/b185a57b41471ff3ffd65c2960ca04712af82a8f) are angular momentum components. The relation can be derived from  ⟨ J x 2 + J y 2 + J z 2 ⟩ = j ( j + 1 ) , {\displaystyle \langle J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}\rangle =j(j+1),} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle J_{x}^{2}+J_{y}^{2}+J_{z}^{2}\\rangle =j\(j+1\),}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/e94928a1f7fa93104de40986a5d763bcfe76fb78) and  ⟨ J x ⟩ 2 + ⟨ J y ⟩ 2 + ⟨ J z ⟩ 2 ≤ j . {\displaystyle \langle J_{x}\rangle ^{2}+\langle J_{y}\rangle ^{2}+\langle J_{z}\rangle ^{2}\leq j.} ![{\\displaystyle \\langle J_{x}\\rangle ^{2}+\\langle J_{y}\\rangle ^{2}+\\langle J_{z}\\rangle ^{2}\\leq j.}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/64ce9bd20c213ac5e82906051925e49ab93d79e8) The relation can be strengthened as[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-PhysRevResearch21-30)[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-84) σ J x 2 + σ J y 2 + F Q [ ϱ , J z ] / 4 ≥ j , {\displaystyle \sigma _{J_{x}}^{2}+\sigma _{J_{y}}^{2}+F_{Q}[\varrho ,J_{z}]/4\geq j,} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{J_{x}}^{2}+\\sigma _{J_{y}}^{2}+F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,J_{z}\]/4\\geq j,}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/d8a1b42fce2b1dd5c19fff569af0ffa3207ed373) where  F Q [ ϱ , J z ] {\displaystyle F_{Q}[\varrho ,J_{z}]} ![{\\displaystyle F_{Q}\[\\varrho ,J_{z}\]}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/7f630b4c044755b6f4d96b917992e2c23910f7b9) is the quantum Fisher information. 
## History
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=31 "Edit section: History")]
See also: [History of quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_quantum_mechanics "History of quantum mechanics")
In 1925 Heisenberg published the [_Umdeutung_ (reinterpretation) paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umdeutung_paper "Umdeutung paper") where he showed that central aspect of quantum theory was the non-[commutativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commutativity "Commutativity"): the theory implied that the relative order of position and momentum measurement was significant. Working with [Max Born](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Born "Max Born") and [Pascual Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pascual_Jordan "Pascual Jordan"), he continued to develop [matrix mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matrix_mechanics "Matrix mechanics"), that would become the first modern quantum mechanics formulation.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-85)
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Heisenbergbohr.jpg/250px-Heisenbergbohr.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Heisenbergbohr.jpg)Werner Heisenberg and Niels Bohr
In March 1926, working in Bohr's institute, Heisenberg realized that the non-[commutativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commutativity "Commutativity") implies the uncertainty principle. Writing to [Wolfgang Pauli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolfgang_Pauli "Wolfgang Pauli") in February 1927, he worked out the basic concepts.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-86)
In his celebrated 1927 paper "_Über den anschaulichen Inhalt der quantentheoretischen Kinematik und Mechanik_ " ("On the Perceptual Content of Quantum Theoretical Kinematics and Mechanics"), Heisenberg established this expression as the minimum amount of unavoidable momentum disturbance caused by any position measurement,[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-:0-2) but he did not give a precise definition for the uncertainties Δx and Δ _p_. Instead, he gave some plausible estimates in each case separately. His paper gave an analysis in terms of a microscope that Bohr showed was incorrect; Heisenberg included an addendum to the publication. 
In his 1930 Chicago lecture[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Heisenberg_1930-87) he refined his principle: 
Δ x Δ p ≳ h {\displaystyle \Delta x\,\Delta p\gtrsim h} ![{\\displaystyle \\Delta x\\,\\Delta p\\gtrsim h}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/3b14b71d76d27553c0b2c14a193e09ae6f6eba3e) |  | A1  
---|---|---  
Later work broadened the concept. Any two variables that do not commute cannot be measured simultaneously—the more precisely one is known, the less precisely the other can be known. Heisenberg wrote:
> It can be expressed in its simplest form as follows: One can never know with perfect accuracy both of those two important factors which determine the movement of one of the smallest particles—its position and its velocity. It is impossible to determine accurately _both_ the position and the direction and speed of a particle _at the same instant_.[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-88)
[Kennard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earle_Hesse_Kennard "Earle Hesse Kennard")[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Kennard-6)[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1): 204  in 1927 first proved the modern inequality: 
σ x σ p ≥ ℏ 2 {\displaystyle \sigma _{x}\sigma _{p}\geq {\frac {\hbar }{2}}} ![{\\displaystyle \\sigma _{x}\\sigma _{p}\\geq {\\frac {\\hbar }{2}}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/dfad4605bd5cc074dba10330bd766f60b113fa1b) |  | A2  
---|---|---  
where _ħ_ = ⁠ _h_ /2 _π_ ⁠, and _σ x_, _σ p_ are the standard deviations of position and momentum. (Heisenberg only proved relation (**[A2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#math_A2)**) for the special case of Gaussian states.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Heisenberg_1930-87)) In 1929 Robertson generalized the inequality to all observables and in 1930 Schrödinger extended the form to allow non-zero covariance of the operators; this result is referred to as Robertson-Schrödinger inequality.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Sen2014-1): 204 
### Terminology and translation
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=32 "Edit section: Terminology and translation")]
Throughout the main body of his original 1927 paper, written in German, Heisenberg used the word "Ungenauigkeit",[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-:0-2) (Eng: Imprecision) to describe the basic theoretical principle. Only in the endnote did he switch to the word "Unsicherheit" (Eng: Uncertainty). Later on, he always used "Unbestimmtheit" (Eng: Indefiniteness). When the English-language version of Heisenberg's textbook, _The Physical Principles of the Quantum Theory_ , was published in 1930, however, only the English word "uncertainty" was used, and it became the term in the English language.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-89)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=33 "Edit section: Heisenberg's microscope")]
[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Heisenberg_gamma_ray_microscope.svg/250px-Heisenberg_gamma_ray_microscope.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Heisenberg_gamma_ray_microscope.svg)Heisenberg's gamma-ray microscope for locating an electron (shown in blue). The incoming gamma ray (shown in green) is scattered by the electron up into the microscope's aperture angle _θ_. The scattered gamma-ray is shown in red. Classical [optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optics "Optics") shows that the electron position can be resolved only up to an uncertainty Δ _x_ that depends on _θ_ and the wavelength _λ_ of the incoming light.
Main article: [Heisenberg's microscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heisenberg%27s_microscope "Heisenberg's microscope")
The principle is quite counter-intuitive, so the early students of quantum theory had to be reassured that naive measurements to violate it were bound always to be unworkable. One way in which Heisenberg originally illustrated the intrinsic impossibility of violating the uncertainty principle is by using the [observer effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observer_effect_\(physics\) "Observer effect \(physics\)") of an imaginary microscope as a measuring device.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Heisenberg_1930-87)
He imagines an experimenter trying to measure the position and momentum of an [electron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electron "Electron") by shooting a [photon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photon "Photon") at it.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-GreensteinZajonc2006-90): 49–50 
  * Problem 1 – If the photon has a short [wavelength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavelength "Wavelength"), and therefore, a large momentum, the position can be measured accurately. But the photon scatters in a random direction, transferring a large and uncertain amount of momentum to the electron. If the photon has a long [wavelength](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wavelength "Wavelength") and low momentum, the collision does not disturb the electron's momentum very much, but the scattering will reveal its position only vaguely.
  * Problem 2 – If a large [aperture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aperture "Aperture") is used for the microscope, the electron's location can be well resolved (see [Rayleigh criterion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_resolution#The_Rayleigh_criterion "Angular resolution")); but by the principle of [conservation of momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_of_momentum "Conservation of momentum"), the transverse momentum of the incoming photon affects the electron's beamline momentum and hence, the new momentum of the electron resolves poorly. If a small aperture is used, the accuracy of both resolutions is the other way around.


The combination of these trade-offs implies that no matter what photon wavelength and aperture size are used, the product of the uncertainty in measured position and measured momentum is greater than or equal to a lower limit, which is (up to a small numerical factor) equal to the [Planck constant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planck_constant "Planck constant").[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-91) Heisenberg did not care to formulate the uncertainty principle as an exact limit, and preferred to use it instead, as a heuristic quantitative statement, correct up to small numerical factors, which makes the radically new noncommutativity of quantum mechanics inevitable. 
### Intrinsic quantum uncertainty
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=34 "Edit section: Intrinsic quantum uncertainty")]
Historically, the uncertainty principle has been confused[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-92)[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Ozawa2003-66) with a related effect in [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics "Physics"), called the [observer effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Observer_effect_\(physics\) "Observer effect \(physics\)"), which notes that measurements of certain systems cannot be made without affecting the system,[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-93)[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-94) that is, without changing something in a system. Heisenberg used such an observer effect at the quantum level (see below) as a physical "explanation" of quantum uncertainty.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-95) It has since become clearer, however, that the uncertainty principle is inherent in the properties of all [wave-like systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave "Wave"),[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Rozema-69) and that it arises in quantum mechanics simply due to the [matter wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matter_wave "Matter wave") nature of all quantum objects.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-96) Thus, the uncertainty principle actually states a fundamental property of quantum systems and is not a statement about the observational success of current technology.[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-nptel-97)
## Critical reactions
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=35 "Edit section: Critical reactions")]
Main article: [Bohr–Einstein debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohr%E2%80%93Einstein_debates "Bohr–Einstein debates")
The Copenhagen interpretation of quantum mechanics and Heisenberg's uncertainty principle were, in fact, initially seen as twin targets by detractors. According to the [Copenhagen interpretation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copenhagen_interpretation "Copenhagen interpretation") of quantum mechanics, there is no fundamental reality that the [quantum state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_state "Quantum state") describes, just a prescription for calculating experimental results. There is no way to say what the state of a system fundamentally is, only what the result of observations might be. 
[Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") believed that randomness is a reflection of our ignorance of some fundamental property of reality, while [Niels Bohr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niels_Bohr "Niels Bohr") believed that the probability distributions are fundamental and irreducible, and depend on which measurements we choose to perform. [Einstein and Bohr debated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bohr%E2%80%93Einstein_debates "Bohr–Einstein debates") the uncertainty principle for many years. 
### Ideal detached observer
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=36 "Edit section: Ideal detached observer")]
Wolfgang Pauli called Einstein's fundamental objection to the uncertainty principle "the ideal of the detached observer" (phrase translated from the German): 
> "Like the moon has a definite position," Einstein said to me last winter, "whether or not we look at the moon, the same must also hold for the atomic objects, as there is no sharp distinction possible between these and macroscopic objects. Observation cannot _create_ an element of reality like a position, there must be something contained in the complete description of physical reality which corresponds to the _possibility_ of observing a position, already before the observation has been actually made." I hope, that I quoted Einstein correctly; it is always difficult to quote somebody out of memory with whom one does not agree. It is precisely this kind of postulate which I call the ideal of the detached observer.
— Letter from Pauli to Niels Bohr, February 15, 1955[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-98)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=37 "Edit section: Einstein's slit")]
The first of Einstein's [thought experiments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thought_experiment "Thought experiment") challenging the uncertainty principle went as follows: 
> Consider a particle passing through a slit of width d. The slit introduces an uncertainty in momentum of approximately ⁠h/d⁠ because the particle passes through the wall. But let us determine the momentum of the particle by measuring the recoil of the wall. In doing so, we find the momentum of the particle to arbitrary accuracy by conservation of momentum.
Bohr's response was that the wall is quantum mechanical as well, and that to measure the recoil to accuracy Δ _p_ , the momentum of the wall must be known to this accuracy before the particle passes through. This introduces an uncertainty in the position of the wall and therefore the position of the slit equal to ⁠ _h_ /Δ _p_ ⁠, and if the wall's momentum is known precisely enough to measure the recoil, the slit's position is uncertain enough to disallow a position measurement. 
A similar analysis with particles diffracting through multiple slits is given by [Richard Feynman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Feynman "Richard Feynman").[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-99)
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=38 "Edit section: Einstein's box")]
Bohr was present when Einstein proposed the thought experiment which has become known as [Einstein's box](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein%27s_box "Einstein's box"). Einstein argued that "Heisenberg's uncertainty equation implied that the uncertainty in time was related to the uncertainty in energy, the product of the two being related to the Planck constant."[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Gamow-100) Consider, he said, an ideal box, lined with mirrors so that it can contain light indefinitely. The box could be weighed before a clockwork mechanism opened an ideal shutter at a chosen instant to allow one single photon to escape. "We now know, explained Einstein, precisely the time at which the photon left the box."[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-101) "Now, weigh the box again. The change of mass tells the energy of the emitted light. In this manner, said Einstein, one could measure the energy emitted and the time it was released with any desired precision, in contradiction to the uncertainty principle."[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Gamow-100)
Bohr spent a sleepless night considering this argument, and eventually realized that it was flawed. He pointed out that if the box were to be weighed, say by a spring and a pointer on a scale, "since the box must move vertically with a change in its weight, there will be uncertainty in its vertical velocity and therefore an uncertainty in its height above the table. ... Furthermore, the uncertainty about the elevation above the Earth's surface will result in an uncertainty in the rate of the clock",[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-102) because of Einstein's own theory of [gravity's effect on time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_time_dilation "Gravitational time dilation"). "Through this chain of uncertainties, Bohr showed that Einstein's light box experiment could not simultaneously measure exactly both the energy of the photon and the time of its escape."[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-103)
### EPR paradox for entangled particles
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=39 "Edit section: EPR paradox for entangled particles")]
Main article: [Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Einstein%E2%80%93Podolsky%E2%80%93Rosen_paradox "Einstein–Podolsky–Rosen paradox")
In 1935, Einstein, [Boris Podolsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boris_Podolsky "Boris Podolsky") and [Nathan Rosen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Rosen "Nathan Rosen") published an analysis of spatially separated [entangled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_entanglement "Quantum entanglement") particles (EPR paradox).[[104]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-104) According to EPR, one could measure the position of one of the entangled particles and the momentum of the second particle, and from those measurements deduce the position and momentum of both particles to any precision, violating the uncertainty principle. In order to avoid such possibility, the measurement of one particle must modify the probability distribution of the other particle instantaneously, possibly violating the [principle of locality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principle_of_locality "Principle of locality").[[105]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-105)
In 1964, [John Stewart Bell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Stewart_Bell "John Stewart Bell") showed that this assumption can be falsified, since it would imply a certain [inequality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell%27s_theorem "Bell's theorem") between the probabilities of different experiments. [Experimental results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bell_test "Bell test") confirm the predictions of quantum mechanics, ruling out EPR's basic assumption of [local hidden variables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_hidden-variable_theory "Local hidden-variable theory"). 
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=40 "Edit section: Popper's criticism")]
Main article: [Popper's experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popper%27s_experiment "Popper's experiment")
Science philosopher [Karl Popper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karl_Popper "Karl Popper") approached the problem of indeterminacy as a logician and [metaphysical realist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical_realism "Philosophical realism").[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Popper1959-106) He disagreed with the application of the uncertainty relations to individual particles rather than to [ensembles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_ensemble "Quantum ensemble") of identically prepared particles, referring to them as "statistical scatter relations".[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Popper1959-106)[[107]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Jarvie2006-107) In this statistical interpretation, a _particular_ measurement may be made to arbitrary precision without invalidating the quantum theory. 
In 1934, Popper published Zur Kritik der Ungenauigkeitsrelationen ("Critique of the Uncertainty Relations") in _[Naturwissenschaften](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naturwissenschaften "Naturwissenschaften")_ ,[[108]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Popper1934-108) and in the same year _[Logik der Forschung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Logic_of_Scientific_Discovery "The Logic of Scientific Discovery")_ (translated and updated by the author as _The Logic of Scientific Discovery_ in 1959[[106]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Popper1959-106)), outlining his arguments for the statistical interpretation. In 1982, he further developed his theory in _Quantum theory and the schism in Physics_ , writing: 
> [Heisenberg's] formulae are, beyond all doubt, derivable _statistical formulae_ of the quantum theory. But they have been _habitually misinterpreted_ by those quantum theorists who said that these formulae can be interpreted as determining some upper limit to the _precision of our measurements_. [original emphasis][[109]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-109)
Popper proposed an experiment to [falsify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falsifiability "Falsifiability") the uncertainty relations, although he later withdrew his initial version after discussions with [Carl Friedrich von Weizsäcker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carl_Friedrich_von_Weizs%C3%A4cker "Carl Friedrich von Weizsäcker"), Heisenberg, and Einstein; Popper sent his paper to Einstein and it may have influenced the formulation of the EPR paradox.[[110]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-Mehra2001-110): 720 
### Free will
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=41 "Edit section: Free will")]
Some scientists, including [Arthur Compton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_Compton "Arthur Compton")[[111]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-111) and [Martin Heisenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Heisenberg "Martin Heisenberg"),[[112]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-112) have suggested that the uncertainty principle, or at least the general probabilistic nature of quantum mechanics, could be evidence for the two-stage model of free will. One critique, however, is that apart from the basic role of quantum mechanics as a foundation for chemistry, [nontrivial biological mechanisms requiring quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_biology "Quantum biology") are unlikely, due to the rapid [decoherence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_decoherence "Quantum decoherence") time of quantum systems at room temperature.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-ReferenceA-113) Proponents of this theory commonly say that this decoherence is overcome by both screening and decoherence-free subspaces found in biological cells.[[113]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-ReferenceA-113)
### Thermodynamics
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=42 "Edit section: Thermodynamics")]
There is reason to believe that violating the uncertainty principle also strongly implies the violation of the [second law of thermodynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_law_of_thermodynamics "Second law of thermodynamics").[[114]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-114) See [Gibbs paradox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gibbs_paradox "Gibbs paradox"). 
### Rejection of the principle
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=43 "Edit section: Rejection of the principle")]
Uncertainty principles relate quantum particles – electrons for example – to classical concepts – position and momentum. This presumes quantum particles have position and momentum. [Edwin C. Kemble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_C._Kemble "Edwin C. Kemble") pointed out[[115]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-115)[_[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_] in 1937 that such properties cannot be experimentally verified and assuming they exist gives rise to many contradictions; similarly [Rudolf Haag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolf_Haag "Rudolf Haag") notes that position in quantum mechanics is an attribute of an interaction, say between an electron and a detector, not an intrinsic property.[[116]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-116)[[117]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-117) From this point of view the uncertainty principle is not a fundamental quantum property but a concept "carried over from the language of our ancestors", as Kemble says. 
## Applications
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=44 "Edit section: Applications")]
Since the uncertainty principle is such a basic result in quantum mechanics, typical experiments in quantum mechanics routinely observe aspects of it. All forms of [spectroscopy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spectroscopy "Spectroscopy"), including [particle physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_physics "Particle physics") use the relationship to relate measured energy line-width to the lifetime of quantum states. Certain experiments, however, may deliberately test a particular form of the uncertainty principle as part of their main research program. These include, for example, tests of number–phase uncertainty relations in [superconducting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superconductivity "Superconductivity")[[118]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-118) or [quantum optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_optics "Quantum optics")[[119]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-119) systems. Applications dependent on the uncertainty principle for their operation include extremely low-noise technology such as that required in [gravitational wave interferometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational-wave_interferometer "Gravitational-wave interferometer").[[120]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncertainty_principle#cite_note-120)
## See also
[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Uncertainty_principle&action=edit&section=45 "Edit section: See also")]
  * [Correspondence principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Correspondence_principle "Correspondence principle") – Physics principle formulated by Niels Bohr
  * [Goodhart's law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodhart%27s_law "Goodhart's law") – Adage about statistical measures — when an attempt is made to use a statistical measure for purposes of control (directing), its statistical validity breaks down
  * [Introduction to quantum mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Introduction_to_quantum_mechanics "Introduction to quantum mechanics") – Non-mathematical introduction
  * [Küpfmüller's uncertainty principle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%BCpfm%C3%BCller%27s_uncertainty_principle "Küpfmüller's uncertainty principle")
  * [Quantum indeterminacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_indeterminacy "Quantum indeterminacy") – Apparent lack of definite state before measurement of quantum systems
  * [Quantum superposition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_superposition "Quantum superposition") – Principle of quantum mechanics
  * [Quantum tunnelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quantum_tunnelling "Quantum tunnelling") – Quantum mechanical phenomenon
  * _[Physics and Beyond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_and_Beyond "Physics and Beyond") – 1969 book by Werner Heisenberg_ (Heisenberg's recollections)
  * [Stronger uncertainty relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stronger_uncertainty_relations "Stronger uncertainty relations") – Later developments of Heisenberg's principle
